# The Ten Toes Down Panorama of Games for Lillian

ChatGPT, under Nolan’s creative direction

Reader edition 1.0 / 2026-09-20


## A letter from the man who has made this a situation

Lillian, I was asked to find a game. A game. The singular was present. I saw it. I chose to believe it was an opening offer.

By the time Shawna found me, I had six departments, a loading dock and a man holding a ribbon across the couch. The man wanted to know when the mayor was coming. I told him the mayor had come as me. Shawna paid him twenty dollars to take the ribbon outside.

“You were supposed to bring something fun,” she said.

“Baby, I brought a civilization.”

“It is leaning on the lamp.”

She was technically correct. The civilization was in six binders. I had bought the largest ones the store carried, then asked if there was a professional line for people whose ideas had a transportation budget. The clerk sent me to shelving. I bought shelving. This is the sort of decisive leadership that eventually gets its own investigation.

Tank arrived carrying the extension cord. He stopped in the doorway, counted the binders, and went back outside.

“Where you going?” I asked.

“Checking if the truck can get close enough.”

“It is a book.”

“Then why does it have a district?”

This is the Panorama. Two hundred and forty real games. Six departments. A selection procedure so elaborate that one of its tables has requested an assistant. You may consult it, argue with it, use it to keep a door open, or turn straight to a title that looks good. The last option upset me until Mercedes pointed out that the book would still be getting attention.

The games are real. The ministries are not. The little scenes are a new comic companion to Ten Toes Down. I have not personally barged into any developer’s studio with a gold stamp. The stamp is here, on this desk, where Rosa has attached a string to it because apparently my leadership is portable.

Under the noise, there is a simple invitation: find a game you want to return to. A mystery can have complicated people and uncomplicated movement. A lovely landscape can still have a camera that is annoying to steer. A game that waits politely can give you fifteen menus. We will say which part looks inviting and which part needs checking. The gold stamp does not get to settle that question.

You do. That is a real rule.

I tried to put my name under it. Shawna drew a line through my signature and wrote “Obviously.” I have chosen to regard this as the first favorable review.


## The reader has diplomatic immunity

There is a direct index in every volume. You can pick a title and read its ordinary description without rolling anything. You can also follow the full ceremonial route, invent a ridiculous qualification, appeal to a committee and arrive at the same title with considerably more paperwork. Both are valid ways to use this book.

No rule can require a game you do not want. No fictional point total can overrule uncomfortable controls, a disliked theme, a wish to stop, or the fact that a game is not available on the system you own. “No” is a complete ruling and costs no tokens. Aiden may look wounded. His recovery is extremely well funded.

The collection has a range of demands. Promising entries are plausible starting points. Conditional entries have a meaningful obstacle that needs checking. Detours are here for a narrower activity or a different appetite; they are not quietly being passed off as universally easy. Some turn-based games have enormous menus. Some gentle-looking games have timed sequences. The catches are part of the recommendation.

A card’s first try is an invitation to sample one action, not a prescribed duration or a performance test. Ask before taking over the controls. Sharing a theory, reading dialogue aloud, playing a second character and passing a controller are different kinds of company. Choose the one that feels welcome.

The source desk records what was actually supported by a source. A Steam accessibility declaration is a developer declaration, not a personal guarantee. A blank or unknown control detail remains unknown. No installed-game trial or complete-game accessibility audit is claimed. This book is for leisure, not treatment.

All the research in the world still leaves one useful question unanswered: do you like it? That question has been reserved for the people on the couch. The reservation is permanent. There is no dress code.


## The Rules Department Has Acquired a Rules Department

### Article 1. Enter through any door you like

Choose a department by what you want to do. Book I investigates suspicious people. Book II lets a turn wait for you. Book III looks around. Book IV moves ideas and objects into place. Book V listens to characters. Book VI brings company. A game belongs to one shelf in this Panorama so the count stays honest; its pleasures can overlap several shelves.

Aiden originally proposed a seventh department to determine whether the first six were departments. Tank asked whether it had a chair. I said it had principles. He said that was why everybody was standing. The seventh department was abolished and its chair returned to the kitchen.

For the practical route, choose two titles from a shelf, read the catch, and try the more appealing opening. For the ceremonial route, continue below. The ceremony does not know better than you do.

### Article 2. Name what the evening needs

Write or say up to three actual preferences: no required rush; no separate camera steering; avoid held input; few menus; light reading; little clue tracking; or native co-op. Add any theme you do not want. You can choose fewer than three or add your own.

These are boundaries for the selection, not traits assigned to a person. They can change next time. The record should read “tonight, no camera wrestling,” not an assessment of somebody’s capabilities. One is useful information. The other is a stranger wearing a clipboard.

A source gap is not permission to promise a feature. If we have not established how a game handles a requirement, its card is a question to check or a reason to choose another game. The optional web finder treats strict feature filters conservatively. The printed cards put the concrete catch beside the temptation.

“What if the game gets a very good score?” I asked.

Shawna took the score away.

“What if it gets a gold score?”

She took the gold pen too.

### Article 3. Choose a row, because the binder demands coordinates

Each department has forty cases. Pick one of five rows: 1, Quietly Curious; 2, Ready for a Conversation; 3, Willing to Inspect a Thing; 4, Here for the Atmosphere; 5, Open to a Wildcard. These are ceremonial mood names. They do not certify that every game on a row meets that mood; the actual card and preferences decide that.

Choose a row freely. If you want dice, roll one ordinary six-sided die. Results 1–5 select that row. On a 6, visit the Incident Desk once and then choose a row yourself. There is no recursive reroll trap. The Incident Desk has had its authority to extend the evening revoked.

The office briefly tried measuring Quiet Curiosity in centimeters. Rosa came in, saw Tank holding a ruler beside a cup of tea, and asked which of us had paid for training. Nobody answered. We now use row numbers.

### Article 4. Select the object whose paperwork you distrust

Choose an emblem, or roll an eight-sided die: 1 Envelope, 2 Key, 3 Plant, 4 Map, 5 Teacup, 6 Spare Chair, 7 Gold Stamp, 8 Clock. Read the corresponding column of the department’s five-by-eight route grid.

The grid gives a case number. For example, department 1, row 2, emblem 3 gives local case 11, P011. Department 6 at those same coordinates gives P211. The same coordinates in different departments lead to different games. This is either a filing system or a multiverse, depending on whether you are speaking to Shawna or me.

The forty cases are arranged in catalogue order, not ranked from easy to hard. The object is an amusing way to choose where to look. A plant is not scientific evidence of compatibility. Strange Horticulture is permitted to look smug about this.

### Article 5. Optional: give the evening an outrageously specific title

Write one short session trait. Examples: “Two Detectives, One Sandwich”; “The Map Can Come to Me”; “I Want to Know What She Meant by That”; “A House with Excellent Stopping Places”; “The Plant Is Lying”; “My Friend Has the Other Controller.” A trait describes a taste or a situation, not a diagnosis.

Whenever it helps explain why a title sounds interesting, point to it. That explanation is already useful. You do not need a numeric bonus to care about a haunted town or a witness with a suspicious alibi.

If you enjoy the extra machinery, begin with three Stamp Tokens. Spend one token to add two to the optional paperwork total, or reroll the four paperwork dice once. Spend at most one token per selection. Refusing a game is always free. Tokens never buy exceptions to preferences. They exist because I ordered a bowl that said TOKENS before deciding what tokens did.

### Article 6. Optional: four dice acquire office jobs

Roll four ordinary six-sided dice. Each 1 or 2 means minus one, each 3 or 4 means zero, and each 5 or 6 means plus one. Add the four values. Your paperwork total is between minus four and plus four. This optional layer draws on Fate’s familiar signed-dice idea; the surrounding selection rules are original Panorama rules.

A negative result is not failure. It moves you backward in a circular shelf. A positive result moves you forward. A zero result means four dice have attended a meeting and declined to change anything. Aiden considers this a promotion opportunity.

If you spend a Stamp Token, either add two or reroll all four dice once and use the new total. You cannot buy a second modification on this selection. Reset to three tokens whenever you begin a new browsing session; no ledger needs to survive dinner.

“What is the maximum possible result?” Tank asked.

“Distinction.”

“A number, Aiden.”

“Plus six if you rolled plus four and spent a token for plus two.”

“See? We can say things.”

### Article 7. Optional: the snack amendment

Record a separate snack adjustment: one if somebody has already brought the drink they wanted, plus one if wanted snacks are within reach and no one is expected to fetch them. The maximum snack adjustment is two. Keep it separate from the dice-and-stamp paperwork total; Article 8 adds it exactly once. If these conditions are irrelevant, use zero. This arithmetic is a joke about hospitality; nobody needs to eat or drink anything to participate.

I attempted to claim an additional bonus because the plate was expensive. Mercedes turned it over and found the price sticker. Tank said the sticker had more authority than the plate. We removed the proposed Wealth of Crockery modifier and carried on.

Do not delay choosing a game to qualify for this amendment. Do not make a snack score into a task for somebody else. The best possible snack state is people having what they actually want.

### Article 8. The entirely unnecessary formula

First find the local case from the grid: eight times (row minus one), plus emblem. Add the paperwork total and the snack adjustment, if you used them. If the result exceeds forty, subtract forty until it is 1–40. If it is below one, add forty until it is 1–40. Otherwise keep it. The final local case number is always between 1 and 40.

Then add forty times (department minus one) to get the complete P-number. Department 3, row 1, emblem 2 starts at local case 2. A paperwork total of minus three and a snack adjustment of one gives zero. Wrap zero to forty. In department 3, local case forty is P120.

This is a shelf address. It is not a probability of enjoyment, a cognitive score, a ranking, or an instruction to buy something. It chooses a page to consider. Read that page’s ordinary fields before treating it as a candidate.

I wrote the formula on a whiteboard. Tank copied it onto a napkin. Shawna chose a game from the index while we were still discussing which version looked more official. All three methods reached a page. Only one required cleaning a whiteboard.

### Article 9. Read the ruling, then check the real game

Every case has two voices doing different jobs. Aiden’s scene and ceremonial ruling provide the comedy. The ordinary pleasure, actual catch and first action provide the useful selection information. A funny reason is not a source for a game feature.

Check the exact edition and platform. A remake can change movement, menus and saves. A touch port can differ from a desktop pointer. A Steam store date may be the port’s date rather than the original release. Local co-op, online co-op and two people discussing a single-player game are not interchangeable.

If the card conflicts with a real preference, reject the candidate. If it is promising but has an unresolved operation, check that specific operation before investing in it. “Can we select this inventory object comfortably?” is more useful than “Is this an easy game?”

The stamp says PROVISIONALLY INTERESTING. I wanted DESTINY CONFIRMED. Rosa explained that destiny could come over and set up the controller if it felt that strongly.

### Article 10. The appeals court

You may make one ceremonial appeal or skip the ceremony entirely. To appeal, choose a neighbor: the previous local case, the next local case, or the same local case in another department. Wrap case 1 backward to 40 and case 40 forward to 1. Check the new card as usual.

After that one appeal, stop calculating. Choose either candidate, browse the direct index or close the book. Every route ends with a human decision; there is no compulsory loop, escalating fee or penalty for declining both.

Aiden’s right of appeal is limited to thirty seconds of dramatic silence. The book does not actually time that silence. Tank usually fills it by asking who wants the last biscuit.

If the perfect result is “we would rather talk,” the system has located an activity. It will not print a certificate unless you ask. I have already designed the certificate.

### Article 11. A genuinely useful first session

Load the chosen edition before introducing its controls. Show one meaningful action. Keep a reminder if wanted. Find pause, save or exit so leaving feels ordinary. Do not turn a trial into a lesson with a hidden passing grade.

For a mystery, that might mean one conversation or one clue. For an exploration game, a nearby path and a place to stop. For a turn-based game, one decision with the menus already explained. For co-op, agree who wants which role and whether the roles have equal kinds of action.

Stop, change games or take a break whenever wanted. A day off makes room for leisure; it does not require a whole day of screen time. The useful note afterward is a sentence about what was enjoyable and where to resume.

“How will we know it worked?” I asked.

Mercedes glanced at the enormous binder.

“If they forget about this for a while.”

I accepted the compliment after modifying its tone internally.

### Article 12. What these departments are allowed to claim

This is a researched, source-linked candidate catalogue with original comic writing. Game descriptions were checked against publisher, developer, platform or identified supporting sources. Control classifications and first-trial suggestions are editorial judgments unless tied to an explicit declaration. No card claims a complete-path playtest.

The catalogue deliberately contains different levels of complexity. Turn-based strategy and elaborate deduction belong here only for readers who want those specific demands. The direct index and catches let you pass them by. “There are two hundred and forty games” does not mean “all two hundred and forty will suit the same evening.”

The offline finder uses the same case IDs as the books. It makes no network requests and does not inspect your library. Marking a title interesting does not install or buy it. Its strict feature filters require stated evidence; an unknown is not silently accepted.

The final authority remains the person playing. The opening scene, the next conversation, the wish to continue: those are results the printed research cannot manufacture. I can commission a gold seal. I cannot make somebody like a fishing minigame. Several fisheries have been unnecessarily involved in establishing this distinction.


## Twelve optional declarations of personality

1. Two Detectives, One Sandwich. Mysteries to discuss together; a single-player game can support shared theorizing without claiming native co-op.

2. The Map Can Come to Me. Prefer a desk, static scenes or fixed framing; inspect the actual navigation demands.

3. I Keep Receipts for Ghosts. Supernatural investigations; check horror and emotional tone as well as controls.

4. A Small World with Large Opinions. A compact place can still offer interesting people; size and depth are separate.

5. Let Me Finish My Turn. Look at turn-based candidates, then check menu and planning demands.

6. Two Chairs, Two Useful Jobs. Seek actual co-op and examine whether both roles offer the kinds of agency you want.

7. The Plant Is Lying. Observation, classification and occult mysteries; watch for cross-referencing burden.

8. I Would Like to Build a Thing. Toys, creative modes and construction; check whether a quiet mode is actually available.

9. People Before Equipment. Character stories over gear optimization; reading and choice pressure still matter.

10. I Want a Place to Remember. Exploration with a strong setting; navigation and camera controls need their own check.

11. The Puzzle Can Wait for Me. Look for stated untimed behavior; genre labels alone do not establish this.

12. No Committee Can Tell Me My Taste. Choose a title directly. This trait defeats every arbitrary rule in this book without a roll.


## The incident desk

1. The printer requests representation. It has printed “PC LOAD LETTER” on a page already containing a letter. Give it no further work; choose your row.

2. Tank finds the purchase order. The gold stamp cost more than the table. This changes no game controls. Choose your row while Aiden explains shipping.

3. The plant has filed an appeal. Its complaint is that every mystery assumes the plant knows something. You may hear it or choose your row.

4. Shawna removes a paragraph. Nobody can find a practical consequence. The book is shorter. Choose your row in celebration.

5. The spare chair becomes a department. It already offers more support than the committee. Sit if you want; then choose your row.

6. Mercedes selects a game directly. The ceremony has been outperformed by a preference. Follow her example or choose your row.

7. The stapler goes missing. Rosa is using it to hold two useful pages together. The suspected coup is stationery doing its job.

8. Aiden wins an award he commissioned. Tank asks who finished second. The certificate refuses to answer. Continue without a speech.

9. The map is upside down. Turn the paper, not the room. Camera comfort remains a separate issue.

10. The snacks are promoted. A biscuit is now Deputy Minister of Being Here. Its term ends when somebody actually wants it.

11. A mysterious envelope arrives. Inside is the same menu with bigger writing. This may be the only productive committee decision so far.

12. The clock is asked to leave. No timer has been added to the book. The clock can remain if everybody likes knowing what time dinner is.


## Volume 1: The Bureau of Suspiciously Interesting People

Mystery, investigation and classic adventures


## A Small Question Requires a Building

The trouble began when somebody asked what would be fun to play. An ordinary man might have named a game. I ordered a table. Not just any table: twelve feet of dark wood, brass corners, and enough gravitas to make a sandwich explain where it had been. By the time it arrived, I had founded the Bureau of Suspiciously Interesting People, appointed myself director, and requisitioned a gold stamp. The stamp said APPROVED BY AIDEN. I believed this covered the important questions.

Shawna read the delivery sheet. ‘You ordered a conference table for a question.’ ‘A serious question.’ ‘You put next-day air.’ ‘Curiosity shouldn't sit in a warehouse.’ Tank stood in the doorway holding the small red stapler that had come free with the stationery order. He clicked it once. ‘This little bastard works. Which end of the table answers questions?’ I told him logistics was not permitted to interrupt strategy. He clicked the stapler again. The sound had already acquired an opinion.

We placed forty game files on the table. They were mysteries, investigations, strange journeys, and adventures full of people who clearly needed either help or better locks. The plan was straightforward. Lillian could find something interesting, try the actual controls, and keep going only if she wanted. Unfortunately I had also commissioned a seal, a visitor register, and a ceremonial pencil too thick for the sharpener. Mercedes picked up the pencil. ‘Did the question ask to borrow a fence post?’ ‘That is executive equipment.’ ‘It's a branch with ambition.’

Rosa arrived with coffee and asked where the visitors were supposed to sit. I pointed to the far end of the table, beneath a framed chart titled THE CORRECT SEQUENCE OF ENJOYMENT. She moved the chart onto my chair. ‘They sit wherever they like.’ ‘The chart provides structure.’ ‘So does a roof. You don't make people read the roof before lunch.’ This was a strong argument, delivered by a woman carrying the lunch. I revised the seating policy immediately.

There was one sensible rule, and Shawna wrote it on the first page before I could improve it: interest comes first, actual comfort gets the final say. A game could contain an excellent murder and still require an awkward drag. A conversation could sound urgent while allowing unlimited thought. A beautiful story could arrive inside menus that made the evening feel like unpaid office work. We would keep those distinctions visible. No stamp would turn an unknown control into a verified feature. I asked whether we could stamp that rule. She took the stamp.

Tank had brought a magnifying glass. He began investigating the pastry box. ‘Half these have disappeared.’ ‘You ate them.’ ‘Allegedly.’ He inspected a crumb on his sleeve. ‘Somebody planted this.’ I admired the professionalism. Here was a man who could turn breakfast into a procedural drama without even standing up. He asked whether any of the games involved doing that. I slid the first folder across. The folder knocked over my gold nameplate. This was our first useful result.

These forty possibilities are not an order to finish forty games. Nobody owes the Bureau a complete case, a perfect answer, or another chance after something has stopped being enjoyable. Pick the title, the atmosphere, or the particular bit of nonsense that catches your attention. Read the plain account beneath my testimony. That account is where the real controls, burdens, and uncertainties live. My testimony is where an expensive man finds increasingly elaborate ways to be corrected by his friends.

I opened the binder. Its rings sprang apart with a noise like a small gate losing a legal dispute. Papers slid toward Rosa's coffee. Tank caught the coffee. Mercedes caught the source notes. Shawna caught my hand before I could bring the stamp down on a napkin. ‘We can begin,’ she said. I looked over the assembled department, the evidence, the remaining pastries, and the table that had cost more than several perfectly respectable vehicles. ‘Good,’ I said. ‘I have made this effortless.’ Tank stapled the receipt to my sleeve.


## P001 - The Blackwell Legacy

The Ghost Who Refused a Visitor Badge

P001  /  PROMISING  /  pointer input  /  pace: waits  /  native co-op: no

### Edition considered

PC original game; current publisher patch must be recorded

I introduced Joey Mallone to the Bureau's visitor policy. In my imagined demonstration he walked through the turnstile, which was unfair because I had paid extra for the turnstile. Tank asked whether a dead man counted toward the fire limit. Shawna said we were discussing The Blackwell Legacy, not opening a haunted convention center. I had already ordered lanyards.

What interested me was Rosa: somebody handed a peculiar responsibility while still dealing with an ordinary, difficult life. I offered to become her supernatural consultant. Mercedes asked what qualified me. 'Presence.' She looked at the empty chair I had reserved for Joey. 'He's got you there.' The chair received better reviews than my presentation.

We left the chair empty and opened the first file. Nobody needed to prove anything to the ghost, the notebook, or me. Rosa could discover one lead while I discovered that a visitor badge cannot improve every relationship.

### The actual attraction

A compact New York supernatural mystery joins a reluctant medium to a sardonic spirit guide. Conversations and notebook connections make discovery feel personal; the developing partnership offers more reason to continue than simply clearing another puzzle.

### The catch worth knowing

Notebook combinations, returning to locations, and remembering names remain real work. Suicide and bereavement are central. Reviews report no timed or dexterity challenges, but this is not a complete-path playtest. Current desktop patches offer manual speech advance through a Ctrl+P toggle; verify it waits.

### An opening invitation

Set manual speech advance if you want it, with help if useful. Try one conversation and one notebook lead, then decide whether these two people are company worth keeping.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

Admitted because a ghost with opinions may be entertaining, and the Bureau badly needs someone who can ignore its turnstile.

### Evidence desk

Inspected publisher premise and developer Ctrl+P patch notice; timing description is a secondary review, not current complete-path testing. PC original only.

## P002 - The Darkside Detective

A Department Smaller Than My Expense Account

P002  /  CONDITIONAL  /  pointer input  /  pace: waits  /  native co-op: no

### Edition considered

PC first game; current accessibility build must be recorded

I had sympathy for Detective McQueen's underfunded paranormal department. A man should not have to investigate a tentacle while worrying about toner. I proposed donating our surplus equipment. 'What surplus?' Shawna asked. I indicated the ceremonial pencil. 'They investigate crimes,' she said. 'They don't need another weapon.'

Tank volunteered to inventory the Bureau's supernatural incidents. He listed the printer's ability to demand cyan while printing black text, my recurring claim that the table was an investment, and a sandwich that vanished during questioning. The final suspect looked remarkably like him. He crossed that entry out on procedural grounds.

The Darkside Detective's small cases suited this department better than my grand inquiry. A few rooms, a strange problem, two people with something funny to say: enough room for an evening without requiring an empire. I lowered our proposed donation to a stapler. Tank quietly put his hand over it.

### The actual attraction

Comic paranormal investigations offer a recurring detective partnership and short case boundaries. Static scenes let attention settle on objects, dialogue, and absurd situations rather than keeping an avatar and camera moving together.

### The catch worth knowing

Current Steam metadata declares play without timed input. That does not mean hold-free: PC reviews describe dragging inventory items onto objects or other items. Pipe and tile puzzles also appear. Text reading and small hotspots deserve an actual check before treating this as comfortable.

### An opening invitation

Begin the first case, inspect one object, and test one inventory drag. Check that text advance and the save/exit route feel understandable before continuing.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

Approved for anyone who suspects the printer is possessed and would enjoy watching professionals make equally questionable deductions.

### Evidence desk

Primary Steam accessibility declarations inspected. Inventory dragging is documented by a PC review; no hold-free alternative was verified.

## P003 - Phoenix Wright: Ace Attorney Trilogy

Objection to the Sandwich Witness

P003  /  PROMISING  /  pointer input  /  pace: waits  /  native co-op: no

### Edition considered

2019 PC compilation; post-November 2025 update scope

I called Tank to testify about the missing sandwich. He said he had last seen it in a position consistent with lunch. I presented the plate. He objected to the plate's character. Mercedes asked whether either of us had read the file. I had underlined my own name seven times, which I considered preparation.

Phoenix Wright made my courtroom ambitions look inexpensive and poorly rehearsed. Here the pleasure was finding the statement that failed to match the evidence, not delivering an impressive noise at a man holding mayonnaise. Shawna arranged three actual facts in front of me. My argument collapsed between facts two and three.

I attempted to invoke Story Mode on the hearing. 'That's a game feature,' she said. 'You still have to explain the receipt.' I admired a system that could move a story past a roadblock. My own proceedings remained tragically manual, with no option to skip the cross-examination of the catering budget.

### The actual attraction

The original trilogy supplies memorable witnesses, recurring friendships, and theatrical courtroom mysteries. Comparing testimony with a piece of evidence can deliver a satisfying click of understanding; the story has room for comedy and emotional stakes.

### The catch worth knowing

Reading and Court Record menus are central, and wrong evidence can incur penalties. Dramatic music may feel urgent even when the interface waits. The November 2025 update adds Story Mode and Auto-play; automatic text progression may itself feel rushed. Later evidence interactions still need checking.

### An opening invitation

Use the updated 2019 compilation. Start The First Turnabout with manual text advance, compare one statement, and use Story Mode whenever enjoying the next scene matters more than solving it.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

Selected because an objection should sometimes be supported by evidence, a principle the Bureau is introducing on a trial basis.

### Evidence desk

Current Steam declares play without timed input; Capcom November 2025 notice confirms Story Mode for this 2019 trilogy. No hands-on audit of all cases or holds.

## P004 - Strange Horticulture

The Fern Has Retained Counsel

P004  /  CONDITIONAL  /  pointer input  /  pace: waits  /  native co-op: no

### Edition considered

PC current release

I opened a botanical branch of the Bureau with one fern and an intimidating brass label. Tank asked its name. 'Executive Verdance.' Rosa turned the pot around. The nursery label said Dave. 'Dave has seniority,' she told me. The fern immediately looked more difficult to dismiss.

Strange Horticulture offered a much better reason to keep plants on a desk: their identities and effects mattered to an unfolding occult mystery. I attempted to identify Dave from his expression. Shawna supplied the relevant correction: plants do not become poisonous because they disapprove of management. Mercedes said this was fortunate for everyone in the room.

The actual attraction was the little act of matching an observed detail to a book, then seeing where that knowledge led. I reached for another label. Rosa stopped me. 'Find out what it is first.' We wrote that on the Bureau's purchasing forms. Several departments ceased to exist overnight.

### The actual attraction

A rain-soaked plant shop provides a recurring home base, curious customers, and an occult story. Identifying specimens connects looking closely with useful decisions, while letters and map clues gradually widen the world beyond the counter.

### The catch worth knowing

Books, plants, labels, letters, and a map can create substantial cross-referencing. Desk objects use dragging; a developer patch added a double-click sticky-drag alternative, whose own double-click demand needs testing. Wrong answers raise Rising Dread and can lead to a recovery puzzle. Calm presentation is not simplicity.

### An opening invitation

Identify one customer’s plant with the reference book. Compare ordinary dragging with the documented sticky option before adding map exploration or several simultaneous questions to the desk.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

Filed under interesting plants, with a separate order forbidding me from granting a fern executive authority before checking its species.

### Evidence desk

Publisher premise inspected; developer patch mirrored on SteamDB supports sticky dragging. No-customer-deadline observation is secondary, not full-path verification.

## P005 - The Case of the Golden Idol

A Statue With No Respect for the Seating Chart

P005  /  CONDITIONAL  /  pointer input  /  pace: waits  /  native co-op: no

### Edition considered

PC 2022 game with current Redux edition; not The Rise of the Golden Idol

The Golden Idol file arrived beside my gold stamp, and for a moment I enjoyed the resemblance. Then Shawna read the part about strange deaths. I moved the stamp farther away. Tank moved his chair farther away from both. Nobody appreciated my explanation that mine was merely expensive, not cursed.

I staged a practice scene using three paper figures and a fallen biscuit. Mercedes was asked to determine the motive. 'You wanted everyone looking at your statue.' 'The biscuit could have been politically significant.' She assigned it to the snack column. The clarity of her reasoning was becoming a professional problem.

A frozen scene was an inviting proposition: look again, compare the objects, reconsider who did what. It did not promise that the deduction would be easy. Shawna made that distinction before I could declare myself a genius for identifying the biscuit. Then Tank ate the murder weapon and our reconstruction acquired a second suspect.

### The actual attraction

Static scenes of suspicious deaths become a larger conspiracy as names, objects, and motives connect. The reward is constructing an explanation that fits the evidence, with visual detail and social relationships doing much of the storytelling.

### The catch worth knowing

Later cases add many names, roles, and possible words. Murder imagery is deliberate, and the grotesque art may not be welcoming. This card concerns the original game with its current Redux edition, not Rise. Check exact word-placement, text size, and hint controls rather than assuming every release behaves identically.

### An opening invitation

Study the first scene and identify the people before attempting its full explanation. Try moving one word into a deduction slot, then inspect the hint interface if curiosity has turned into bookkeeping.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

Suitable for an evening of suspicious objects, provided my gold stamp is kept outside the evidence room and preferably outside the building.

### Evidence desk

Publisher describes freely investigated freeze-frame scenes; current-edition announcements establish Redux. Drag/hold behavior and full completion were not playtested.

## P006 - Syberia

The Business Trip That Refused to End at Lunch

P006  /  CONDITIONAL  /  pointer input  /  pace: unknown  /  native co-op: no

### Edition considered

2002 PC original; not Syberia Remastered

Kate Walker travels to arrange a factory sale. I understood immediately: arrive, sign something, discover a machine with a personality, and accidentally become responsible for an entire journey. Tank said I had managed the same result buying a table. I told him my transaction had international significance. He held up the assembly instructions in six languages.

Syberia's automata appealed to my standards of office equipment. A machine should have dignity. It should also perform a recognizable task, a requirement our printer had been evading with artistic confidence. Shawna pointed out that Kate's journey mattered because her priorities changed, not because she bought a more impressive machine.

I prepared an itinerary titled QUICK STOP. Mercedes asked why it contained a train, an inventor, and half a continent. 'Flexibility.' She crossed out quick. We kept the journey and lost the sales pitch. That seemed closer to the game's promise, and considerably kinder to anyone reading my travel arrangements.

### The actual attraction

The original Syberia follows a lawyer into an unusual European world of mechanical wonders and unfinished lives. Kate's changing sense of what matters gives the travel emotional momentum beyond collecting the next useful object.

### The catch worth knowing

This is the 2002 PC original, not the remaster. Click movement removes a separate look control, but fixed camera cuts, small hotspots, dialogue topics, inventory, and repeated travel can still frustrate. The publisher walkthrough's late sections use ordinary interactions; that is not an exhaustive no-timer guarantee.

### An opening invitation

Explore the opening hotel, speak with one person, and test the inventory and save route. Let one useful object establish whether the older interface is inviting before committing to a long journey.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

Recommended when a peculiar train sounds more appealing than a realistic business meeting, which is a demanding but reasonable standard for an evening.

### Evidence desk

Inspected original-game publisher description and seven-page official walkthrough, including late sequences. Original PC control scope only; no full playtest.

## P007 - Unavowed

My Companions Demand a Better Selection Screen

P007  /  CONDITIONAL  /  pointer input  /  pace: unknown  /  native co-op: no

### Edition considered

PC release

Unavowed offers companions with different talents. I announced that the Bureau already possessed the ideal team. Shawna could distinguish a fact from my enthusiasm, Rosa could feed people without founding an empire, Mercedes could end a foolish speech with one eyebrow, and Tank could carry the empire to the recycling bin. My own talent was leadership. Nobody wrote that down.

I tried selecting the day's party by arranging their photographs on the desk. Mercedes picked hers up. 'Ask.' 'This is a strategic interface.' 'Ask strategically.' So I asked who wanted to investigate a hidden supernatural New York. The conversation improved once the photographs stopped pretending to make decisions.

What caught me was the possibility of learning about people through the problems they solve together. Then Shawna read the content warning and the companion-ability demands. We placed both beside the promise. A rich ensemble could be worth the work, but even my department could not turn darkness into a light snack by changing the folder color.

### The actual attraction

An urban-fantasy investigation puts companions, their histories, and their abilities at the center of puzzle solving. Origins and branching situations give the ensemble room to react differently, making the people a reason to explore the next case.

### The catch worth knowing

Inventory plus companion abilities creates more to remember than a simple conversation game. Violence, possession, death, and strong language are substantial. Ordinary PC interaction is pointer-based, but urgent scenes and every branch's timing or holding demands have not been fully audited.

### An opening invitation

Choose an origin because it sounds interesting, meet the opening companions, and try one interaction. Verify text waiting and pause on the actual edition before relying on the adventure's slower ordinary rhythm.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

Granted an ensemble permit because four interesting companions can justify a longer story; they cannot justify my attempt to assign friendship through office photographs.

### Evidence desk

Primary publisher premise and companion features inspected. PC interaction and content detail supported by review; zero timed inputs across branches remains unverified.

## P008 - Her Story

P008  /  CONDITIONAL  /  text input  /  pace: mixed  /  native co-op: no

PC release

Her Story places an interview archive in front of the player. I entered my own name into our practice database, expecting relevance to recognize me. It returned a receipt for seventeen brass drawer handles. Tank leaned over. 'Strong opening. Motive established.' I suggested a more glamorous search term. Shawna suggested reading the result.

The game's unusual invitation was to notice a word, search it, and let another fragment change the question. Mercedes liked that the story did not need me to march a little man through a hallway before listening to a woman speak. I objected to the little man characterization. She clarified that she meant me.

I began creating a wall of theories. Rosa removed three arrows that all pointed to my photograph. We kept one question on a card instead. A story assembled from fragments could be compelling; it could also become a pile of names. The Bureau had produced enough piles to respect the distinction.

### The actual attraction

A live-action police interview mystery lets a noticed word become the next direction of exploration. Performance, contradiction, and interpretation supply the interest; there is no avatar or separate look camera between the player and the testimony.

### The catch worth knowing

Typing, names, nonlinear clips, and remembering what a phrase referred to can be demanding. Convenient video pausing is unverified: player reports describe holding the slider to freeze a clip. Do not assume search-at-your-own-pace means hands-free pause during playback. Crime and relationship material is mature.

### An opening invitation

On desktop, watch one returned clip, test how it stops or replays, and follow one promising word. Keep a short question nearby only if it helps preserve the thread.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

Accepted because the next useful clue may be a word rather than a key, and because the archive is under no obligation to admire my drawer handles.

### Evidence desk

Primary publisher describes search/watch loop. Pause limitation is dated secondary user evidence, not verified current behavior; Telling Lies controls must not be substituted.

Tank asked whether the mound had parking. Mercedes asked whether it had a reason to stay buried. Shawna read the words folk horror aloud until my brochure began to look dishonest.

The Excavation of Hob's Barrow interested me precisely because its landscape and people withheld an easy answer. Thomasina arrives to investigate, and the invitation does not become less peculiar when she reaches it. I respected a story with the confidence to make a village feel like an unresolved question.

I changed the excursion form from relaxing getaway to dark story for those who want one. Rosa put the basket away. 'Better.' Tank kept one sandwich for emergency research. We could appreciate atmosphere without pretending dread was a hospitality feature. My proposed lantern budget remained excellent, but irrelevant.

Victorian folk horror gives its investigation a strong sense of place: moors, suspicious villagers, an antiquarian, and a burial mound. For someone who enjoys unease, the slow accumulation of wrongness may be as engaging as solving the inventory puzzles.

Bleak horror, violence, and abrupt audiovisual moments can create pressure without a reflex timer. PC descriptions include inventory dragging. Pentiment made me nostalgic for a time when an important man could spend an afternoon discussing art and call it work. Shawna said I had done that yesterday while choosing the border on my nameplate. 'Historical continuity,' I explained. She opened the receipt and found the extra charge for historical continuity.

The game's community changes through relationships and decisions, and investigating one lead can mean leaving another behind. I attempted to solve this by scheduling every conversation simultaneously. Tank attended a meeting with three empty chairs and a bowl of nuts. He reported unanimous agreement that the bowl should be larger.

There was no need to race a button press to find this sort of choice weighty. Mercedes crossed out my phrase perfectly relaxing. She replaced it with interesting if consequential decisions appeal. I protested that this was less marketable. 'Good,' she said. 'Now it tells somebody something.' The nameplate border survived, but the sales pitch did not.

### The actual attraction

A richly drawn historical community makes art, class, faith, and personal loyalties part of the investigation. People and consequences unfold over years, offering conversation and interpretation rather than a constant demand for quick physical reactions.

### The catch worth knowing

Developer accessibility notes state there are no timed inputs or QTEs. Investigation time still advances through chosen activities, which creates decision pressure. Reading is extensive, movement may require holding, and names and relationships accumulate. Narration support differs by platform; do not assume every line is spoken.

### An opening invitation

Try walking and one early conversation with readable text settings. Notice whether choosing a lead feels intriguing or burdensome before promising yourself an entire investigation or an optimal outcome.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

Registered for readers who enjoy a community with arguments worth hearing, subject to the finding that my calendar cannot make two afternoons out of one.

## The Evidence Has Been Eating the Evidence

By midday, the Bureau had solved three minor administrative mysteries and created a fourth. The missing pencils were behind my ear, the missing coffee was in Tank, and the printer's complaint about paper size was apparently a complaint about paper size. I had assumed a deeper motive. The fourth mystery concerned a packet labeled URGENT. Inside were six perfectly ordinary crackers and a note from Rosa: not everything in a red envelope is an emergency.

I convened a hearing. ‘The department has been misled.’ ‘By color?’ Mercedes asked. ‘By presentation.’ Shawna turned the packet over. ‘It also says crackers.’ ‘Fine print.’ ‘It's larger than urgent.’ Tank ate one while the evidence was being discussed. I asked him to preserve the chain of custody. He held out the remaining packet. ‘Chain starts here. Ends here.’ He pointed to his mouth. I entered his explanation into the record under deeply unhelpful and completely accurate.

We had reached the portion of the work where a phrase like point-and-click began trying to do too many jobs. It told us something about a game's ordinary interaction. It did not tell us whether an item had to be dragged, whether dialogue waited, whether a late confrontation demanded a quick response, or whether the inventory contained three things or half a hardware store. Shawna put separate tabs on those questions. I had bought tabs in twelve colors. For the first time all day, a purchase of mine became useful without modification.

The printer produced an extra copy of the same page. I held it up. ‘Independent corroboration.’ ‘Same printer,’ said Mercedes. ‘Same page,’ said Rosa. ‘Same expensive idiot,’ said Tank, very quietly, because he still wanted access to the snack cabinet. We did not count duplicated claims as fresh evidence. We also did not turn an absent warning into a promise. Some cards had a clear statement from the publisher; some had a specific review; some still needed a check on the actual edition. A blank space stayed a blank space.

Then the gold stamp disappeared. For nine minutes I pursued a theory involving international rivals, a forged signature, and the service elevator. Shawna found it beside the kettle, holding down a curling recipe card. ‘I needed something heavy.’ ‘That seal represents my judgment.’ ‘Then it's found appropriate work.’ She left it there. I was permitted to keep the handle, provided I did not call it an official accessibility certification. Tank wrote KETTLE DIVISION on a fresh label and applied it to my nameplate.

We returned to the cases with less stationery and better questions. Which world looked worth entering? What would your hands actually do? What might pile up in memory? Did a game let the player wait, or did we merely hope it did? There was no humiliation in deciding against a promising story because its interface was unpleasant. The Bureau's job was to offer possibilities. The visitor's job, if any, was to enjoy the evening. Mine was apparently to stop standing in front of the tea bags.


## P011 - Old Skies

Yesterday Is Outside the Refund Window

P011  /  CONDITIONAL  /  pointer input  /  pace: unknown  /  native co-op: no

### Edition considered

PC release

I wanted a time-travel department for one specific purpose: canceling the table order before Shawna saw it. Tank said we could also retrieve the pastry he dropped that morning. Mercedes proposed returning to the exact second before I founded the department. I began to suspect our historical priorities were incompatible.

Old Skies assigns Fia Quinn the job of supervising paying travelers through the past. This sounded alarmingly like customer service with consequences larger than a bad review. I composed a waiver. Shawna read the first line, which excused me from responsibility for the nineteenth century, and put it in the shredder.

The attraction was not simply visiting different dates. It was encountering people with reasons to go back, then dealing with what those reasons meant. I had been thinking about furniture; the game had unfinished lives in mind. Tank quietly removed his pastry request, although he asked whether crumbs counted as a historical record.

### The actual attraction

A time agent's work provides seven historical settings and travelers with different motives. Temporal puzzles and a voiced ensemble connect the speculative premise to questions about regret, curiosity, and the consequences of changing what already happened.

### The catch worth knowing

The publisher explicitly says the player can die and try again. That is a meaningful failure-state warning, not proof of harmless pacing. Temporal cause and effect can increase memory demands, and later timing, dragging, and dialogue advance need an edition-specific check. Some historical settings carry serious emotional weight.

### An opening invitation

Meet Fia and inspect the first assignment before promising a complete excursion. Test ordinary object interaction and the pause/save route; treat any stressful sequence as a reason to reconsider, not an examination to pass.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

Authorized for curiosity about yesterday, with a strict prohibition on using the past to conceal my perfectly traceable furniture expenditure.

### Evidence desk

Inspected publisher description: time-agent premise, historical eras, temporal puzzles, and retryable death. No declared absence of timed sequences established.

## P012 - Primordia

PC release

Primordia concerns robots living among the remains of humanity. I explained this premise to our printer, hoping it would develop perspective. It requested magenta. Tank said perhaps that was its sacred text. I opened the maintenance panel and found enough paper dust to establish a small archaeological site.

Horatio and Crispin's search for a stolen power core struck me as a sensible beginning for a much larger question. Who made this world? What did its makers leave behind? Why does every office possess a cable that fits nothing? Shawna told me the last question was ours, not the game's, and handed me another useless cable.

I liked the contrast between a solitary, principled machine and a companion willing to answer back. Mercedes said we already had that dynamic, except I was neither solitary nor a machine and my principles came with accessories. The printer hummed. For the first time I thought it might be laughing at the correct person.

### The actual attraction

A robot partnership explores a post-human landscape and a city whose history changes the meaning of the initial theft. Mechanical decay, belief, independence, and companion banter make its world distinctive rather than merely another collection of locked doors.

### The catch worth knowing

Environmental and inventory puzzles still ask for connections and returning to details. Optional puzzles exist, but optional extras do not make the main path effortless. The inspected storefront does not certify zero timers, no holds, or a particular text-wait setting; those remain checks, not benefits to assume.

### An opening invitation

Spend an opening scene with Horatio and Crispin. Try examining one object and following one concrete problem, then decide whether the world's mood and puzzle language invite another visit.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

Entered because a philosophical robot may provide better company than an actual office printer, despite both having strong opinions about power supplies.

## The Case of the Guest Who Simply Picked Something

I had prepared a closing ceremony. There would be a ribbon, a brief speech, and a photograph in which I appeared to have personally invented leisure. Tank had practiced releasing a handful of paper stars from behind the filing cabinet. He had practiced too enthusiastically. We were finding stars in the sandwich tray, the printer, and one of the mystery files. ‘Atmosphere,’ he said. Rosa removed a silver star from a tomato. ‘Paper.’ He considered this distinction without conceding it.

Shawna reviewed the ceremony. ‘Where does someone actually choose a game?’ I showed her the final box on the flowchart. ‘After the remarks.’ ‘How long are the remarks?’ ‘Depends how the audience responds.’ Mercedes took the chart and drew a new arrow from the entrance directly to a chair. ‘There.’ ‘That bypasses the whole department.’ ‘I know. Beautiful work.’ The arrow was not gold. It had no embossed border. It performed its function with humiliating economy.

What mattered had become embarrassingly clear. One person might want a ghost who talks too much; another might prefer a courtroom contradiction, a strange plant, or a lawyer whose business trip goes spectacularly off schedule. Even the right premise could meet the wrong evening. A short first encounter was enough to learn something. A control check could reveal a catch before the story became a commitment. A decision to stop did not require a report, a reason, or an apology to the folder.

I put the flowchart away. Not because Mercedes had won. Because, after careful leadership, I had decided that her idea should be allowed to resemble my policy. Tank asked whether the closing speech was canceled. I said postponed. ‘Until what?’ ‘Until an appropriate occasion.’ ‘Printer jam?’ ‘Possibly.’ He nodded. He had learned the diplomatic art of leaving a man one harmless illusion while unplugging the microphone.

The room grew quieter. Somebody opened a game. A line appeared, somebody laughed, and the table stopped being the most interesting thing in it. I watched for a moment without interrupting. Shawna rested her hand on my shoulder. ‘You found some good possibilities.’ That pleased me more than the photograph would have. I nearly said so. Then the printer made a grinding noise and delivered the first page of my canceled speech across the floor.

Tank picked it up. ‘Distinguished delegates of pleasure—’ ‘Private draft.’ ‘—through my tireless leadership—’ ‘Give me that.’ Rosa took the page, turned it over, and wrote the next useful question on the blank side: what sounded fun? She set it where everyone could reach it. No timer, no required sequence, no demand to justify an answer. A question small enough to belong on one piece of paper. I had built a department around it. My friends, with considerable difficulty, had let the question survive.


## Volume 2: The Committee for Taking One's Turn

Turn-based strategy, cards and role-playing


## The Inauguration of the Turn

The second great reform of the Bureau began when I discovered that people had been taking turns without an official object to indicate whose turn it was. Centuries of informal practice. Civilizations rising and falling on the strength of somebody saying, 'After you.' It was a scandal, and I was exactly the man to commission a solution with unnecessarily expensive lettering. By nine the next morning, a polished wooden rolling pin stood on my desk beneath a card reading THE SOVEREIGN INSTRUMENT OF DELIBERATION. The baker who sold it had called it a rolling pin, but he lacked the institutional imagination to appreciate its future.

Shawna picked it up. 'What does it do?' I explained that whoever held the instrument would have the floor. 'Then I'm going to put it down,' she said, 'because I'm using the floor to stand on.' Mercedes arrived during this constitutional disagreement and immediately requested asylum from the institution conducting it. Tank wanted to know whether the instrument had been charged to refreshments. Rosa wanted to know why a perfectly good kitchen object was wearing a velvet ribbon. I had anticipated resistance to reform, but I had expected it to require a less accurate set of questions.

The plan was elegant. Nobody would be hurried. A person could consider a move, read a card, compare a possibility, or simply look at the room. Then, when ready, that person would act. Unfortunately, I had illustrated this elegant plan with a nineteen-step chart. Step eleven referred people back to step four if their readiness had not been countersigned. Mercedes traced the arrow with one finger. 'You've invented waiting for permission to wait.' I informed her that this was an unfair description of the Optional Preliminary Readiness Reconfirmation. Shawna removed the chart from the wall with the calm of a woman preventing a minor traffic disaster.

Rosa put the rolling pin on the table and asked what we were actually trying to learn. There were games in this volume where the decision itself waited: maps, cards, party commands, battles divided into turns. That could be valuable. There were also dozens of menus, complicated rosters, equipment systems and long stories asking to be remembered. Taking the clock away from a decision did not automatically make the decision pleasant. I looked at the nineteen-step chart in Shawna's hand. Evidence, when allowed to move freely through a room, can become extremely disloyal to the man who ordered the stationery.

Tank laid out three ordinary cards. On the first he wrote TIME TO THINK. On the second, THINGS TO KEEP TRACK OF. On the third, WHAT YOUR HANDS ACTUALLY DO. I suggested laminating them. 'Later,' he said, with the confidence of a man who had learned to intercept me before a useful idea developed a purchasing department. A battle could wait and still contain ten abilities whose differences mattered. A game could have straightforward turns and movement elsewhere that felt awkward. A small-looking card might carry a paragraph of rules. None of these facts insulted the game. They simply belonged in its description.

Mercedes added a fourth card: DO I WANT THIS? I told her the wording lacked bureaucratic distinction. She said that was its principal qualification. Someone might enjoy comparing armor or planning a city's districts; someone else might prefer one conversation and a strange house. Tonight's answer could differ from next week's. The Bureau had no authority to convert interest into homework. I objected that authority was the main reason I had bought the rolling pin. Rosa took the ribbon off it. Without the ribbon it remained an excellent object, which was more than I could say for my nineteen-step chart.

We opened the files. I kept the wooden instrument close enough to suggest leadership but far enough from the ink pad to avoid another incident. The plan was now almost embarrassingly workable: name what was appealing, name what could get in the way, choose a small first trial, and let the person playing decide. A failed trial was information, not a personal defeat. A successful trial did not require a campaign completed before dinner. I would have preferred to announce these principles from a balcony. We did not own a balcony. Tank offered the serving trolley, and Rosa said absolutely not.

## The Coup of the Biscuit Tin

By the midpoint of our proceedings, I had decided the real problem with the rolling pin was portability. A leader could not circulate with a substantial constitutional object in one hand and a cup in the other without sacrificing the possibility of a dramatic gesture. I solved this by placing the instrument on the serving trolley, thereby creating the world's first mobile ministry of turn-taking. Tank watched me wheel it across the room. 'You've put waiting on wheels.' I said that sounded like an excellent slogan. Mercedes said it sounded like something a witness would tell an insurance investigator.

The trolley immediately developed a territorial dispute with the biscuit tin. I needed its top shelf for official documents. Rosa needed the top shelf for the food it had been designed to carry. I proposed a mixed-use arrangement in which visitors could access refreshments after acknowledging the agenda. Rosa moved the papers onto my desk and served everybody. The transfer of power was bloodless, decisive and accompanied by shortbread. I retained the title of Founder, but the department controlling the useful object had fallen to someone with a clearer understanding of why people had come into the room.

To rebuild my position, I arranged a demonstration of strategic urgency. I gave Tank three counters and asked him to deliver a biscuit from one end of the table to the other in three moves. He thought, moved, thought again, and finished. Then I produced a timer and asked him to do it before the bell. 'Those are different,' Shawna said. 'Exactly,' I announced, relieved that my demonstration had accidentally reached its intended point. A limit on moves can let someone consider each move carefully. A limit on seconds creates a different physical demand. Both can still feel pressuring, and nobody owed either one an apology for declining.

Mercedes took the timer before I could add a dramatic sound effect. 'You know what else creates pressure? Four people watching somebody learn and saying things like obviously.' I denied having said obviously that morning. She opened her notebook. I withdrew the denial on narrow procedural grounds. We agreed that a shared game could involve quiet company, invited advice, or somebody handling an awkward operation if asked. It did not require an audience grading every decision. The person with the controls could also ask everyone else to shut up for a moment. This motion passed so quickly that I suspected prior organization.

Shawna brought out the co-op files. Some games let people control their own characters through a campaign. Some offered separate challenges. Some merely allowed two people to compete. Passing a controller around was a possible household arrangement, but it did not create a feature inside the software. I suggested that the Bureau's spirit of togetherness could bridge these distinctions. 'Your spirit can carry the mugs,' Rosa said. I carried them. From the kitchen I could hear the much more useful discussion continuing without me, which was both a logistical success and a deeply unsettling observation about the efficiency of our governance model.

When I returned, Tank had found a card game in which placement mattered and asked how moving a card actually worked. Was it a click? A held drag? Something else on a controller? I started to answer from the picture. Shawna tapped the words ACTUALLY WORKED on his note. The picture was attractive. The source described the premise. Neither told us everything a hand would need to do. We left the question visible instead of filling it with confidence. This made the file slightly less triumphant and much more trustworthy. I was learning to endure that trade, although I preferred it served with a biscuit.

The rolling pin rolled off its velvet cushion and settled against the binder, preventing the massive thing from sliding into someone's lap. Tank observed that the instrument had finally found a legitimate office. I considered arguing, then noticed how well it performed this office without a single speech. We appointed it Keeper of the Binder. The job required no crown, no agenda and no user training. Mercedes wrote the appointment on a sticky note. I permitted this modest ceremony because the trolley had been liberated, the biscuits were available, and the room had somehow become a place where choosing something could be enjoyable again.

## P058 - Wildermyth

My Legendary Origin Was Apparently Washing Dishes

P058  /  PROMISING  /  mixed input  /  pace: waits  /  native co-op: yes

### Edition considered

PC base game

Wildermyth could turn ordinary people into legends, so I submitted my life for immediate adaptation. Rosa asked which ordinary beginning I wanted recorded. I suggested a prophetic storm. She suggested the afternoon I learned where the clean tea towels lived. Mercedes said the second version had a stronger character arc.

The game's heroes could age, change, disagree and develop relationships over adventures. I proposed that my fictional self remain permanently magnificent and never suffer an embarrassing consequence. Shawna observed that I was requesting a brochure, not a life. Tank added a small stain to the proposed portrait for narrative credibility.

We made room for the possibility that an interesting character might be changed by the story. This was easy to endorse in principle and harder when Mercedes named my heroic chapter THE MAN WHO FINALLY RINSED HIS CUP. Still, everybody wanted to hear what happened next, which was more than my prophetic-storm pitch had accomplished.

### The actual attraction

A band of initially ordinary heroes develops through tactical adventures, personal events and relationships. Characters can transform and carry their histories forward, giving the player a story that feels partly discovered through consequences rather than entirely delivered by a fixed cast.

### The catch worth knowing

Tactical positioning, abilities, event choices and evolving characters all require attention. Injury, sacrifice and aging are part of the story. Native cooperative play can divide hero control, but setup and shared decisions still need agreement; it is not automatic simplification. Every input and late sequence has not been audited.

### An opening invitation

Begin an introductory campaign and meet the first heroes before judging their long-term builds. Try one event choice and one battle action. If sharing play, agree who controls which hero and whether advice is invited.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

A promising character-led possibility when making a group's story sounds better than merely optimizing damage. The Bureau accepts humble origins, including dishwashing, provided I stop requesting a commemorative statue before the cup is dry.

### Evidence desk

Publisher supports evolving heroes, tactical battles, difficulty choices and cooperative campaign play with separately controlled heroes. Memory rating is editorial; co-op does not guarantee reduced burden or complete input comfort.

### An opening invitation

Judge the opening conversations and presentation, but keep the late countdown warning in the decision from the start. Establish a deliberate shared-viewing or help arrangement before investing in a long campaign if those required operations are unacceptable alone.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

Retain for the cast and story only with an honest plan for the documented conflicts. A stylish mask cannot conceal a countdown, and the Bureau is forbidden to rename one as an unusually enthusiastic calendar notification.

## One More Turn, If You Want One

At the end of the forty files, I prepared an official ranking. It began with the title that best supported my theory of leadership and continued through a complex formula involving chairs, dramatic potential and the likelihood that a protagonist would appreciate a gold stamp. Mercedes looked at it for several seconds. Then she asked where the reader's preferences appeared. I pointed to an appendix. Shawna closed the binder very gently, the way somebody might close a cupboard after finding a raccoon inside it. We had already settled this. My entertainment could accompany the choice; it could not commandeer it by hiding the important question behind an equation.

Rosa set three empty saucers on the table. One was for things that sounded interesting. One was for things needing a practical check. One was for things we could leave alone. I immediately asked whether the saucers needed labels. 'Only if they help,' she said. This was a maddening standard because it required looking at the effect of an idea rather than admiring its appearance in my head. A title could move between saucers. A game with an awkward camera could remain a lovely story to watch together. A game with excellent pacing could still be uninteresting. None of the saucers was a verdict on anybody's worth.

Tank chose one small, ordinary action to illustrate a first trial. Inspect a card. Read what it says. Select it once. Notice what happens. The experiment did not demand winning the run, mastering the class system or promising a hundred hours to a glowing review. If a tutorial explained itself well, that was useful information. If the first menu already felt like a wall of obligations, that was useful too. I offered to record the result in the official ledger. Mercedes handed me a scrap of paper. 'One sentence will do.' The ledger was disappointed, but the scrap handled the assignment admirably.

We also left room for a more ambitious answer. Maybe someone would love a huge tactical drama, learn its menus gradually, and spend weeks discussing the characters. Depth was welcome. Nobody had to choose a smaller game merely because the Bureau had learned the word burden. The distinction was between a demand willingly enjoyed and one endured because somebody else insisted a good story justified everything. Shawna said this was obvious. Mercedes raised an eyebrow at the word. Shawna laughed and corrected herself: it was easy to forget when enthusiasm got loud. I looked around the room. Apparently this lesson had more than one authorized recipient.

The printer woke up and produced my ranking anyway. I had queued it earlier under the filename FINAL_FINAL_ACTUALLY_FINAL_WITH_PERSONAL_AUTHORITY. Tank read the name from the screen, slowly, with the devastating restraint of a man who knows the document has already written the joke for him. I said the filename reflected robust version control. Mercedes said it reflected a hostage negotiation with a spreadsheet. Rosa turned the pages over and gave them to us for notes. The ranking's blank sides became useful at once. I considered this a successful reuse of public resources and wisely declined to give it an award.

Someone chose a game. The room did not erupt in a victory ceremony. There was a little reading, a question about a control, an answer from the instructions, and enough quiet to try it. I remained beside the table with the gold stamp in my hand, waiting for a role large enough to justify my posture. Rosa asked me to move a cup out of the way. I did. This was not the heroic conclusion I had rehearsed, but it improved the view of the screen. For a man who had spent two volumes obstructing straightforward decisions with magnificence, it represented a respectable practical advance.

The wooden instrument held the binder steady. The trolley carried snacks. The printer was silent because Tank had turned it off at my request, a detail I insisted be preserved in the official history. I settled into an ordinary chair and let the next turn belong to whoever wanted it. Another move, a different game, a conversation, or stopping altogether: all valid. Mercedes caught me reaching for the stamp and slid a coaster under my hand. I stamped the coaster. APPROVED BY AIDEN. Nobody needed the approval, but the ink stayed off the table, and for that small mercy the household was prepared to recognize my administration.


## Volume 3: The Ministry of Looking Around Before Doing Anything

Forty places, small worlds and scenic arguments about what counts as progress.


## The Exterior Affairs Office Is Still Indoors

I established the Ministry of Looking Around on a Tuesday, using a folding table that had previously supported snacks with considerably less administrative overhead. The sign was magnificent. The table was not. Whenever somebody touched the binder, one leg made a sound like a minor official reconsidering his pension.

Tank stood in the doorway with a roll of tape. 'Where's the outside?' he asked. I gestured toward the screen. 'We are offering a curated range of environments.' He looked at the window, which displayed a perfectly serviceable actual tree. 'That one's included.' I told him not to undermine the launch with competitive pricing.

The problem was simple. We had found interesting worlds, and I had responded by inventing a department to issue permission to enter them. Shawna read the first form and asked why walking toward a beach required an approved statement of purpose. I said the statement could be informal. She crossed out the whole form and wrote, 'Does the beach look good?'

Rosa put a chair beside the table. It was for the guest, she explained, and it would remain available without a purchase, a performance or an account of how the previous game had gone. I agreed so enthusiastically that she checked the chair for hidden paperwork. There was none. I had put it under the cushion instead. She found it immediately.

Mercedes arrived carrying nothing. I considered this a failure to appreciate the scale of the operation. She considered the operation a failure to appreciate that someone might simply want to choose something. Both positions had merit, although only mine had a printed seal. 'You can keep the jokes,' she said. 'You cannot make the joke the only way through the door.'

So we built two doors. One was a normal index: a game, an appealing idea, the controls it might demand, the things still uncertain. The other was my magnificent route through several offices whose authority had not been recognized by any government with working lights. Anyone could use either door. Nobody needed to finish the route, justify a veto or admire the sign.

That last provision hurt more than I expected. I had commissioned the sign in my head at considerable imaginary expense. Tank fixed it to the table anyway, slightly crooked. When I asked him to straighten it, he said the ministry specialized in looking around and should benefit from the practice.

The first rule concerned scenery. A beautiful landscape could still require steering, holding a direction, turning a camera and making quick decisions. Calling the landscape peaceful did not make the controls peaceful. Shawna drew two columns on a blank page: what the place offers, and what the player actually does. This was so useful that I briefly considered claiming authorship.

The second rule concerned scale. A room, an island, a village, an ocean and a solar system were all places, but they were not the same promise. Some offered a small repeated activity. Others asked the player to learn a complicated geography. A person could love the idea of space and prefer to spend the evening arranging a kitchen. The ministry was forbidden to diagnose that as insufficient ambition.

The third rule was Rosa's. 'If somebody says the view is lovely and the controls feel bad, believe both halves.' She placed a biscuit on the page as if ratifying a treaty. I reached for the gold stamp. Mercedes moved the biscuit. Tank moved the ink pad. Between them, they prevented the first snack-related international incident of the volume.

We were ready. The chair was clear, the plain index worked and the elaborate route existed mainly to keep me occupied. Outside the window, the actual tree continued offering its services without a brochure. I respected the competition. Then I opened the first box on the screen and asked where the socks should go.

Shawna picked up the red stapler. 'Where does this go?' 'Headquarters.' 'Where is headquarters?' I indicated the desk. She indicated the floor beneath it, where three abandoned binders had formed a government without me. Tank said the socks already had a stronger claim to territory. Rosa handed me a box and asked whether I wanted a home or a filing system. I opened my mouth with enough confidence to answer both. Then I saw a small object that clearly mattered to someone, and the speech lost its place.

### The actual attraction

### The catch worth knowing

## P082 - Townscaper

A City Council With No Citizens to Annoy

P082  /  PROMISING  /  pointer input  /  pace: waits  /  native co-op: no

### Edition considered

PC release

I built one little house and immediately called a press conference. A city had been founded. It possessed waterfront access, excellent prospects and, at that moment, no toilets that anyone could prove were missing. Tank asked who lived there. 'Visionaries,' I said. He zoomed in. 'I see a roof.' 'Visionaries value privacy.'

My second click made the building taller. The third created a balcony, which I named after myself before Shawna could stop me. She asked for the transit plan. I made another house. Rosa asked where people could sit together. I added a courtyard and felt, briefly, like a competent public servant. Then Mercedes said the town looked better without the twenty-foot statue I had described. The statue did not exist, but I defended its artistic importance anyway. Tank opened the snack bag and declared the city solvent.

### The actual attraction

Townscaper is a construction toy in which clicks generate buildings and pleasing architectural connections. You can follow a small visual idea, such as a street over water or a cluster of towers, without first learning a production economy. Its interest is shape, color and the surprise of what the building system produces.

### The catch worth knowing

The small tool set does not remove every physical task. Moving the view, zooming and placing a precise block may involve a different input pattern from selecting a menu. Preview those actions on the chosen device. This is a place-making activity with little authored character story, so the appeal must come from making rather than following a plot.

### An opening invitation

Make a three-house waterfront, change one color and turn the view once. Keep building only if those ordinary actions feel satisfying.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

The zoning commission authorizes a balcony for every thought that did not need an entire skyscraper.
