The first landscape tile contained a railway, so I declared the nation connected. 'Connected to what?' Tank asked. I laid down another tile with a forest. 'Nature.' He waited while I rotated the piece three times. 'The train appears to terminate in a tree.' I explained that this was a premium woodland arrival experience.

Shawna wanted to know whether we were building for a score or building because the little countryside looked good. I said those were inseparable elements of my development philosophy. She opened the mode menu and separated them in five seconds. Rosa put a biscuit beside the keyboard and requested a river with a place to imagine sitting. That was an achievable public demand. I stopped trying to make the railway impress a committee and began making the water bend around a village. Nobody thanked my previous railway policy, which was probably fair.

### The actual attraction

Dorfromantik assembles a landscape from hexagonal tiles. Fields, settlements, forests and waterways make the next placement visually meaningful. ### The catch worth knowing

Choose the mode deliberately. Standard play involves tile supply, objectives and scoring; Creative mode is a separate assessment target, not a label for every session. Rotation, camera movement, small connection details and interpreting the interface still need checking. The sources support the game's building premise, not a universal promise that every control is hold-free.

### An opening invitation

Open Creative mode with setup help if needed. Place one village tile, one field and one stretch of water, then examine the connections without chasing a score.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

The railway may end in a forest, provided nobody calls it a national emergency.

A little path curved toward the shoreline, and Rosa followed it with her eyes instead of consulting my document. There was someone to talk to down there. I reminded her of the summit reception. Shawna asked who had confirmed attendance. I said the summit had an excellent reputation for remaining in place. Mercedes folded the itinerary into a shape that looked uncomfortably like a paper airplane. For a moment I considered confiscating it. Then the game offered a lovely view and an ordinary conversation, and I realized the mountain had not actually employed me. I sat down and let the path be a path.

### The actual attraction

A Short Hike offers a compact island, a climb toward a destination and small encounters along the way. The developer describes an unhurried journey, but the controls still ask for movement. Climbing and gliding use held input; gliding also requires steering. Those demands matter even when the mood is gentle. Side activities need their own check, and there is no native simultaneous local co-op in the assessed version.

### An opening invitation

Walk along an early shoreline or marked trail and speak to one character. Introduce climbing and gliding only after ordinary movement has become comfortable.

Summit attendance is optional; the shoreline has already accepted your reservation for absolutely nothing.

## The Camera Comfort Summit

I convened the summit after discovering that I had used the word relaxing three times without mentioning how anyone would move. Shawna arrived with the offending pages. Tank arrived with a mug. Rosa arrived with the ordinary expectation that a meeting should eventually improve something.

'Imagine a lovely village,' I began. 'I can,' Mercedes said. 'Now tell me what my hands are doing.' I described the village again, using better adjectives. She waited. It was the sort of silence that turns an expensive sentence into a small unpaid debt.

Shawna put a controller on the table and separated the actions. Moving was one thing. Turning the view was another. Aiming at an object could be a third. Sometimes the game combined them; sometimes stopping before turning helped; sometimes the whole arrangement remained unwelcome. The summit had been called to record that distinction, not to argue a person out of noticing it.

I proposed a badge reading APPROVED BY THE COUNCIL OF BEAUTIFUL HORIZONS. Rosa suggested CAMERA CHECK FIRST. Mine had grandeur. Hers had information. We used hers.

Tank asked whether the same principle applied to holding buttons. It did. A peaceful forest could ask for a long climb. A friendly photograph game could ask for awkward panning. No amount of soft music could click the mouse for you. We wrote the actual action beside the actual attraction.

By the end, the summit had produced a short checklist and no compulsory exercise. Mercedes called it a success. I called it the first session of a continuing international initiative. Shawna crossed out continuing. Tank ate the remaining biscuit before I could appoint it ambassador.

## P097 - ABZU

P097  /  CONDITIONAL  /  camera input  /  pace: mixed  /  native co-op: no

### Edition considered

PC release

I entered the water with the confidence of a man who had never attempted to run an office underwater. Within seconds, my imaginary papers had become an expensive soup. Tank asked whether the fish needed to complete a visitor form. I said we were modernizing toward verbal admission. The fish did not answer.

There were colors everywhere, movements within movements, and no obvious place to hang a portrait of the director. Rosa followed a school of fish with genuine interest. Shawna tested the controls instead of accepting my claim that floating was basically sitting with ambition. Mercedes said the view was enough to justify being quiet for a moment. I managed nearly the whole moment. Then a much larger shape passed nearby, and all my prepared remarks became irrelevant. The ocean, I discovered, had been operating successfully without a reception desk for an embarrassingly long time.

### The actual attraction

ABZU makes underwater movement and marine life the center of a largely visual journey. Swimming among schools of creatures and discovering changing seascapes can be absorbing without a dialogue-heavy plot. Its attraction is the feeling of entering a place that moves around you.

### The catch worth knowing

Swimming in three dimensions can require substantial steering and camera coordination. The official description also warns of dangers in the depths, so a peaceful visual style is not proof of uniformly pressure-free play. Check whether turning, ascending, descending and stopping are comfortable before committing to the wider journey.

### An opening invitation

Use the first suitable open-water area to practice a short forward swim and a gentle turn. Let a companion observe the controls rather than immediately taking over.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

The fish have unanimously approved your presence and unanimously declined to become a committee.

## P098 - Journey

The Stranger Has Arrived Without a Business Card

P098  /  CONDITIONAL  /  camera input  /  pace: mixed  /  native co-op: unknown

PC release

A traveler appeared, and I prepared to ask which department had sent them. They supplied no credentials. No résumé. Not even a tasteful embossed card. Tank said perhaps we could simply travel in the same direction. I found this dangerously unstructured, although it did eliminate the need for a printer.

The ruins stretched ahead. Movement and music did more explaining than my orientation packet ever had. Rosa liked the possibility of company that did not arrive with an obligation to perform. Mercedes pointed out that I was giving an unusually long speech about an unusually quiet experience. Shawna lowered my imaginary microphone. For once I followed someone without turning it into a leadership exercise. We crossed a little distance together, and I did not need to know their job title. Later I wrote 'colleague' in the visitor book, then crossed it out and wrote 'person.'

### The actual attraction

Journey builds meaning through landscape, movement, music and the possibility of encountering another traveler. Its sparse presentation leaves room for the player to interpret the trip. This may appeal when the desired story is a feeling of passage or companionship rather than detailed dialogue and inventory puzzles.

### The catch worth knowing

The experience still involves steering, gliding and moving through a three-dimensional environment. The possibility of another traveler is online companionship, not a promise of local couch co-op or a reliably present helper. Check camera comfort and later pressure before treating its quiet opening as representative of every scene.

### An opening invitation

Cross one early area and notice whether movement and the visual direction feel natural. Decide whether the atmosphere is engaging before worrying about reaching the destination.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

The stranger may accompany you without submitting a biography, a loyalty statement or a photograph suitable for the newsletter.

It could see farther, travel faster and had never once ordered a binder that blocked a doorway. Tank asked whether I would accept an advisory position. 'Senior advisory,' I said, because dignity should not be abandoned casually.

The hunter moved with a confidence my office could not manufacture. Shawna watched the archery and asked the only useful question: would those actions be enjoyable to perform? I began describing the landscape. She waited. Rosa said admiring a game and wanting to operate it were two separate pleasures. Mercedes offered to put that sentence on the wall, replacing my certificate of exceptional relevance. I agreed, reluctantly. The eagle circled overhead with the expression of someone who had already approved the motion. Some leadership transitions happen without a meeting.

### The actual attraction

The Pathless combines a hunter, an eagle companion and a large mythic landscape. Movement and archery are part of its identity, alongside exploration of ruins and the story of a curse. It belongs here as a visually appealing action detour for someone specifically curious about that kind of movement.

### The catch worth knowing

The primary description explicitly includes high-speed archery, acrobatics and boss battles. This is not an ordinary no-rush recommendation. The developer's forgiving treatment of permanent damage or death does not remove required reactions. Camera, aiming and combined inputs are central checks, and a strict no-reaction preference should send the reader elsewhere.

### An opening invitation

Watch a short official gameplay sequence together, then test basic traversal only if that motion looks appealing. There is no obligation to turn admiration into play.

## P100 - Feather

I Have Become a Bird and Still Requested a Window Seat

P100  /  CONDITIONAL  /  camera input  /  pace: mixed  /  native co-op: yes

### Edition considered

PC release

I took flight and immediately asked where the passenger lounge was. Tank reminded me that I was the aircraft. 'Then the service should improve.' Shawna suggested learning to turn before filing a complaint about the sky. It was a reasonable suggestion delivered with the confidence of someone who had seen me attempt parallel parking.

The island had places to circle and sounds to discover. Rosa liked the absence of enemies. Mercedes liked that no one appeared to be asking for a quarterly result. I tried to name a cloud after myself, but it moved away before the paperwork could be completed. That felt healthy for the constitution. After a few turns, I stopped treating the air as a route between meetings and began noticing the movement itself. Tank said I looked peaceful. I ruined it by requesting a commemorative feather, but only after a respectable interval.

### The actual attraction

Feather offers bird flight around an island, with music and changing scenery as the reward. The developer explicitly describes a world without enemies, combat or threats and supports flying alone or with online players. Its appeal is movement for its own sake, rather than collecting equipment or following a character drama.

### The catch worth knowing

The absence of threats does not make flight effortless. Continuous steering, orientation, camera motion and any held controls need a practical check. Online multiplayer is different from native local co-op. Someone who wants a substantial mystery or a clear sequence of tasks may find the open-ended flight less satisfying than the premise suggests.

### An opening invitation

Try one broad circle above an open area. Check turning and camera comfort before exploring narrow passages or inviting another online flyer.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

Your window seat has been upgraded to the entire sky; catering remains a matter between you and the nearest tree.

## The Minister Attempts to Monetize a View

The ocean page gave me an idea. We could issue scenic appreciation certificates. A bronze certificate for noticing a tree, silver for noticing a mountain and gold for making a thoughtful sound near water. Tank asked whether 'huh' qualified. I said it depended on the water.

Rosa stared at me long enough for the idea to begin retreating on its own. 'Why does enjoying something need proof?' I explained that the certificates were fictional. 'So is the fee,' she said, 'but you still made the reader think about paying it.' This was a fair criticism of a scheme that had existed for less than a minute.

We replaced the certificates with a return note: one place, one person, one unanswered question. The note was optional. It could help someone return later without reconstructing a whole evening. It could also remain blank. Mercedes insisted that blank was a legitimate state for a piece of paper, which threatened several of my departments.

Shawna added a line about stopping. A game could be left at a pleasant moment, before mastery, before completion, even before my dramatic explanation reached its conclusion. I felt that last example was unnecessarily personal.

Tank tested the system by closing the binder in the middle of my sentence. Nothing bad happened. The room remained intact. The snacks were still present. When he opened it again, the page had remembered where it was without requiring an apology.

I withdrew the certificate program. The printer expressed no opinion, but I like to think its silence contained relief.

There was no obvious list to conquer, which left my stamp unemployed. Rosa walked toward something because it looked interesting. Mercedes listened. Shawna asked whether I could allow an experience to be worthwhile without collecting a certificate at the end. I said certainly, then checked whether there was a certificate. The colors shifted; the surroundings made music out of our presence. Gradually I stopped waiting for the place to explain my importance. It was a peculiar relief. Tank later asked what I had accomplished. I told him I had been somewhere. He nodded like that was a complete answer.

### The actual attraction

Proteus is an exploratory island experience in which the surroundings respond musically to where you go. It has little conventional instruction, text or plot. The pleasure comes from observation and the changing relationship between sound, color and place, rather than solving a case or completing a set of chores.

### The catch worth knowing

Its minimal goals can be either freeing or frustrating. First-person movement and camera control remain necessary even when there are few menus. The lack of hints is a documented design choice, so someone who wants a clear next action should treat that as a meaningful catch. There is no basis here for promising that an unstructured world will feel easy.

### An opening invitation

Walk toward one visible landmark and listen to what changes. If the absence of a stated objective feels empty rather than inviting, choose a more directed game.

P105  /  CONDITIONAL  /  mixed input  /  pace: mixed  /  native co-op: no

PC release

I looked at the house and recognized a familiar problem: somebody had continued adding things after the sensible stopping point. Tank looked at my binder. Neither of us said anything for a moment. Then I announced that I respected ambitious structures, provided they did not expect me to carry them.

Each room suggested a person rather than merely another section of the floor plan. Rosa asked me to stop calling the house an asset. Shawna warned that the way we interacted might change as the stories changed. Mercedes said a family cannot be understood by arranging its members into a tidy chart. I had already drawn the chart, naturally, but its lines began to look inadequate. We stepped toward another story. I put the ruler away. The house did not become less strange, but its strangeness started to feel like somebody's attempt to keep a person from disappearing.

### The actual attraction

What Remains of Edith Finch uses a remarkable family home to connect a series of short, varied stories. The attraction is the inventive relationship between each person's life and the way their story is presented. It offers rich emotional material without requiring the player to manage a conventional role-playing character sheet.

### The catch worth knowing

Variation is also the principal control catch. The developer explicitly says the gameplay changes between family stories, so learning the opening does not establish a stable interaction pattern for the whole game. First-person navigation and later combinations need checking. Death is central throughout; this is a content preference to consider, not a surprise to conceal behind the attractive house.

### An opening invitation

Explore an early room and assess the first change of interaction. Continue because that style of storytelling interests you, not because the opening seemed mechanically simple.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

The architect is excused from making the family sensible; the player is excused from finishing a story whose emotional weather is unwelcome.

## The Day the Map Became a Tablecloth

I printed a map of the ministry's proposed travel routes and discovered that it was larger than the table. This was not a printing error, I explained. It represented the generosity of our scope. Tank said scope was hanging over the side and touching his shoe.

Shawna asked what decision the map helped someone make. I pointed to a branching route through clouds, machines, islands and philosophical robots. She asked what happened if someone only wanted a small place to explore. I traced three lines, lost the third beneath the snack bowl and admitted that the map had developed ambitions unrelated to its job.

Rosa turned it over. The blank side made a surprisingly good tablecloth. Mercedes wrote three options in the center: a room, a short walk, a larger world. Under each, Shawna added the control check that mattered. The result was less majestic and dramatically more usable.

I objected that forty games could not possibly fit into three options. Mercedes said they did not have to. The options were ways into the catalogue, not cages around it. A person could choose a page directly, change interests halfway through or decide the best destination was the kitchen.

Tank put the snack bowl back. This time it obscured no essential information. I considered naming the tablecloth after myself, then noticed that the ministry had finally achieved a rare kind of elegance: the complicated thing had become furniture for the simple thing.

We kept the reverse side available for anyone who wanted to admire the absurd map. It was funny. It was also optional. Those two facts improved each other.


## P111 - INFRA

The developer explicitly describes hazards and a fight for survival despite the absence of guns. This is therefore an investigative detour, not an automatic calm-walking recommendation. ## A Window That Does Not Require an Appointment

By closing time, the ministry had sent nobody anywhere against their wishes. I regard this as an administrative triumph, although Rosa said it was merely the minimum standard for hosting someone in a room.

The cards had offered boxes, villages, forests, birds, machinery, houses, deserts and a solar system with a thoroughly uncooperative schedule. Some were promising because their documented interactions matched the desired pace. Others were conditional because the beauty came with a camera, a held movement or a puzzle structure worth checking. A few were detours, present because admiration and curiosity are broader than immediate suitability.

Shawna stacked the pages without ranking the people who might choose them. There was no ladder from respectable games to lesser ones. There were different pleasures and different demands. A small toy could hold an evening. A grand narrative could be exactly wrong. A game could be interesting today and unwelcome tomorrow without anybody needing to defend the change.

Mercedes opened the plain index and selected a page without consulting my route. I experienced a brief constitutional crisis. Then I remembered that this was precisely what the route had been built to permit. The institution was working best when it made itself unnecessary.

Tank took the crooked sign down and leaned it beside the window. The actual tree was still there. I asked whether we should include it in the next edition. Rosa said only if I could describe it without inventing a user account.

We left a clear chair, an ordinary note and three possible starting points. The binder remained large, but it no longer blocked the doorway. That was progress worth noticing.

I reached for the gold stamp one last time. Shawna looked up. I stamped a scrap of paper instead of a requirement: YOU MAY JUST LOOK. Tank said the tree had already communicated that more efficiently. I agreed, which startled everybody enough to end the meeting.


## Volume 4: The Department of Things That Fit Into Other Things

Puzzles, mechanisms, creative toys and retro desktop diversions


## The Cabinet Refuses the Binder

I bought the cabinet because its label said STORAGE SOLUTION. I considered this a binding professional promise. The cabinet arrived flat, which was the first sign that it expected me to participate in the solution. Tank carried the pieces inside and laid them next to the table. Shawna found the instructions. I found the decorative nameplate I had ordered separately and placed it where the finished cabinet would eventually have a chest.

'You bought a nameplate before checking the measurements,' Mercedes said. 'A institution needs an identity,' I replied. 'It also needs to fit through the doorway.' Rosa brought tea, examined the wall and quietly moved a vase beyond the area in which my confidence was most likely to cause damage. Tank held two boards together. The pieces looked deceptively simple. There were only a few shapes, a small bag of fittings, and an unlimited number of ways for me to explain that the problem was somebody else's.

The binder was supposed to go in the top compartment. It did not. I turned it sideways. The cabinet declined. I removed a page, which reduced the thickness by an amount Tank described as emotionally significant. Shawna asked why I had started assembling a storage system around an object without measuring the object. I told her the binder represented a living vision. She handed me a tape measure. 'Then let's see how long it lives.'

This was the moment the Department of Things That Fit Into Other Things discovered its subject. A puzzle can use a few familiar actions and still ask a wonderfully difficult question. A quiet board can offer deep reasoning. A richly rendered cabinet can conceal a control that needs a held drag. An attractive cartoon can contain a severe logic problem, while a serious-looking mechanism can become perfectly readable once somebody shows you what moves. Appearance and physical demand had been filing reports in the same folder. Shawna separated them.

I proposed three labels: BRILLIANT, EXTREMELY BRILLIANT, and AWAITING MY ARRIVAL. Mercedes crossed them out. Rosa wrote two actual questions on an index card: Do I want to know what happens? Does doing it feel good? Tank added a third: Is Aiden standing on the instructions? I stepped aside. Under my shoe, a diagram showed the shelf fitted the other way around. The cabinet had not been refusing the binder. It had been refusing my interpretation of a rectangle.

We agreed that the book could contain difficult reasoning without pretending every difficulty was the same. A game could wait patiently while a person considered a complicated relationship. Another could demand a quick movement despite its thoughtful story. A puzzle about changing rules might be exciting; a menu full of tiny icons might merely be tedious. The job was to say which activity was actually on offer, identify the catch, and leave the decision with the person who would play.

I tried to turn this agreement into a certification scheme. Shawna said a useful description would do. I tried to add a compulsory introductory lecture. Rosa said the tea would get cold. Mercedes selected a page directly, bypassing my entire route system, and the floor remained under us. Tank fitted the shelf correctly. The binder still protruded a little, but the cabinet now served a purpose. I attached the nameplate on the inside, where its authority would interfere with fewer people.

## P124 - Snakebird

The Bird Has Exceeded the Approved Length for Birds

P124  /  CONDITIONAL  /  directional input  /  pace: unknown  /  native co-op: no

### Edition considered

PC original game

I requested a bird of standard dimensions. The bird ate fruit and became longer, which I considered an unauthorized amendment to the seating plan. Tank suggested reserving two chairs. Then it grew again. My hospitality model was losing ground to produce.

Shawna asked what the extra length made possible. I had been so busy declaring it inconvenient that I had not considered usefulness. Rosa watched the shape cross a gap in a way a shorter creature could not. Mercedes said a constraint sometimes becomes a tool if you stop insisting it should be something else. I wrote that down, then discovered I had used the entire width of the page. Tank offered a longer sheet and waited for the irony to reach me. Eventually it did. We returned to the puzzle with a better question: not how to make the bird ordinary, but how to use the bird we had.

### The actual attraction

Snakebird makes the length and shape of its fruit-eating creatures central to spatial puzzles. What looks like a small comic animal becomes a tool for crossing, supporting and arranging a route. The attraction is the way a simple change in body length changes the whole problem.

### The catch worth knowing

The developer explicitly calls the game deceptively challenging. A friendly drawing and a small board should not be sold as easy reasoning. Check directional input, switching between creatures and recovery from mistakes. The card does not establish universal no-timing behavior from genre alone; its strongest case is for a player who enjoys concentrated spatial experimentation.

### An opening invitation

Try the earliest fruit-and-exit arrangement. Notice what the new length permits before attempting to plan several moves ahead.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

The bird may exceed the furniture specification; the reader may decline the puzzle without submitting a shorter bird.

### Evidence desk

## P125 - Snakebird Primer

The Orientation Bird Has Brought a Smaller Problem

P125  /  PROMISING  /  directional input  /  pace: unknown  /  native co-op: no

### Edition considered

PC separate prequel game

After our first encounter with an extremely long bird, I commissioned an orientation program. It contained a twenty-page introduction to the concept of fruit. Shawna removed nineteen pages. Tank ate the visual aid on the remaining one. We were forced to begin with the actual game.

The new island introduced its ideas with less apparent hostility to our afternoon. Rosa appreciated having room to learn a pattern before the pattern acquired relatives. Mercedes said a gentler introduction did not make the activity lesser; it made the invitation clearer. I agreed, then tried to call myself the architect of this educational philosophy. Shawna pointed to the developer's name. I withdrew the claim and concentrated on the creature in front of us. One small success led naturally to another question. This was a much better orientation than my speech about the historic significance of the banana.

### The actual attraction

Snakebird Primer is a separate set of puzzles designed as a more approachable introduction to Snakebird's spatial ideas. The developer explicitly contrasts its easier island with the original game's serious challenges. It can be a useful place to discover whether moving and shaping these creatures is pleasurable before choosing the harder title.

### The catch worth knowing

Easier is a comparison, not a personal guarantee. Directional movement, visual planning and keeping track of a changing shape still matter. Check undo, restart and control repetition in the exact release. The appeal remains puzzle logic rather than rich character dialogue, and a player need not graduate to the original game to justify enjoying this one.

### An opening invitation

Complete or explore one introductory arrangement, focusing on how fruit changes the creature. Let curiosity about the next idea decide whether to continue.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

The orientation certificate is optional, the banana is gone and nobody has been enrolled in mandatory advanced bird studies.

## P126 - A Good Snowman Is Hard To Build

The Snowman Has Asked Me to Stop Calling Him a Deliverable

P126  /  PROMISING  /  directional input  /  pace: waits  /  native co-op: no

### Edition considered

PC release

I set a target of three snowmen before lunch. Rosa asked why. 'A department requires outputs.' She looked at the small monster on the screen, then at me. 'Maybe the monster wants to make one nice snowman.' Tank supported this position, partly because lunch was already approaching without any help from my target.

Shawna studied how the snowballs occupied the space. Mercedes wondered what would happen if I stopped measuring the afternoon by completed figures. I tried making one deliberate move. The little arrangement became interesting in itself: size, position and the order in which things could happen. My production chart began to look ridiculous beside it. I turned the chart over and drew a face on the back. Tank said it was my most useful contribution so far. I gave the face a senior title anyway, because reform is a gradual process.

### The actual attraction

A Good Snowman Is Hard To Build makes a small, affectionate premise into a spatial puzzle: a monster creating snowmen. The compact scenes invite attention to a few objects and how their positions relate. It can offer a satisfying repeated activity without needing a sprawling world or a dense inventory story.

### The catch worth knowing

The current developer listing declares play without timed input and keyboard-only support. Check movement, undo and snowball manipulation on the chosen device; those declarations do not establish that every action avoids holding. The puzzles still require planning and trial, so the gentle presentation should not become a promise of effortless solutions. Narrative is limited to the charm of the activity.

### An opening invitation

Try making one snowman and notice where your first plan changes. Learn the recovery control before treating a mistaken move as a reason to restart everything.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

The snowman is recognized as a pleasant use of an afternoon and exempted from quarterly productivity reporting.

### Evidence desk

Exact Steam developer description supports premise. Current developer declarations include Playable without Timed Input and Keyboard Only Option. Physical comfort, held inputs and reasoning load still need their stated checks; this is not a hands-on completion test.

## P127 - Cosmic Express

PC release

I promised the aliens a world-class transit system. Then I discovered the passenger car could carry only one of them at a time. Tank asked whether my promise had included capacity planning. I said the service emphasized intimacy. Shawna began drawing a route that actually reached the houses.

Every turn in the track mattered. Rosa considered the passengers as destinations rather than decorative obstacles. Mercedes asked me to stop calling a loop around nowhere a scenic service. I erased the loop. Another route almost worked, which was somehow more interesting than my first confident answer. We watched the small consequences accumulate until the problem became clear. The station did not need a grand announcement. It needed a track that delivered somebody home. I canceled the ribbon ceremony and spent the saved time looking at the final corner. This improved transport more than any of my slogans.

### The actual attraction

Cosmic Express asks you to lay railway routes through small space stations and deliver their residents. The one-passenger constraint makes the order of visits important. Its appeal is the clean relationship between a visible route and the consequences that follow from it.

### The catch worth knowing

The developer describes the puzzles as difficult and separately declares play without timed input, with keyboard-only and mouse-only options. Drawing tracks may still involve sustained pointer actions, so test the intended input method. Cute passengers do not reduce the planning burden: a compact station can hold a demanding puzzle. The declared input options do not certify hold-free interaction.

### An opening invitation

Draw a route in an early station and watch one passenger's trip. Test editing or resetting the route before trying to optimize a complicated arrangement.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

The railway is authorized to be small, provided it goes somewhere more useful than the minister's opening speech.

It produced a concrete result, unlike my lecture. The next island held another object and another chance to be amused by certainty. I quietly changed my proposed museum plaque from 'Authoritative History' to 'A Person Had a Guess.'

### The actual attraction

A Monster's Expedition combines tree-pushing spatial puzzles with comic museum observations about humanity. Small islands form a larger exploratory structure, so solving a route can lead to both a new place and a new joke. It offers a particular mix of thoughtful manipulation and dry amusement.

The developer describes simple mechanics with substantial depth. That depth can mean difficult planning even when the action vocabulary is small. Check movement, undo, reset and the readability of the islands and exhibits. Its open structure should not be treated as proof that every puzzle is optional or that every route remains easy.

### An opening invitation

Bonfire Peaks presents a spatial puzzle journey organized around closure and moving onward. Its compact, sculptural environments make objects and their placement visually central. It may appeal when the satisfaction of an exact arrangement is strengthened by a quiet theme about what people carry.

The primary description promises rewarding challenges and evolving mechanics, not low difficulty. Manipulating objects in three-dimensional puzzle spaces may require careful orientation and planning. ### An opening invitation

Inspect an early puzzle, learn one object interaction and test how to recover from a mistake. Continue only if the precise arrangement feels absorbing rather than burdensome.

Shawna noticed the hint system. Instead of simply handing over an answer, it offered a smaller playable idea. Rosa liked that: a nudge that still left discovery in the player's hands. Mercedes asked whether my own explanations could become shorter and more useful in the same way. I said yes, then took four sentences to prove that I had not yet achieved it. We tried the small hint puzzle. Something clicked. The larger problem changed from a wall into a question we could work with. I appointed the tiny puzzle head of training and accepted a probationary role.

### The actual attraction

Can of Wormholes develops many interactions from a small collection of puzzle objects. Its distinctive strength for this catalogue is a documented playable hint system: a smaller related stage can suggest the insight needed for the larger one. Experimentation and discovery are the main rewards.

### An opening invitation

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

The training department is approved because it offers a useful smaller idea instead of requiring attendance at my worm-related keynote.

## The Undo Budget

I established an Undo Budget after noticing how often a puzzle could become interesting precisely when an earlier idea turned out to be wrong. The proposal included a request form, a committee and a modest administrative charge payable in public acknowledgment of my foresight. Tank asked whether we could undo the proposal. I said that would require the form. Shawna tore the form in half. Apparently the department had discovered a direct-action interface.

Rosa put two of the cabinet's spare fittings on the table. She moved one, considered the result, then put it back. 'That's all,' she said. 'Sometimes trying an idea is how you find out what the problem is.' Mercedes added that a mistake should not automatically become a performance review. I had been preparing a report on the fitting's lack of ambition, so this intervention saved everyone several pages.

Undo and restart still needed to be checked in the actual game. We could not award them by wishing, and we could not assume that a feature in one installment existed in another. But when a useful reversal was present, it changed the feeling of experimentation. A move could be a question. An unsuccessful arrangement could leave behind a better understanding. Nobody had to preserve the first idea simply because I had stamped it.

I attempted to undo my purchase of the oversized binder. Tank said that feature belonged to the shop's returns policy, not the puzzle. We found a practical alternative: divide the contents into smaller volumes and keep the route card visible. It was not a spectacular solution, which made it unusually easy to explain. I claimed the improvement as a triumph of iterative leadership. Mercedes asked whether she could undo that sentence. We let her.


## P131 - Braid, Anniversary Edition

I Would Like to Rewind the Part Where I Announced Expertise

P131  /  DETOUR  /  directional input  /  pace: real time  /  native co-op: no

### Edition considered

PC Anniversary Edition

The ability to reverse time struck me as the most promising administrative reform in history. I could retract an entire speech without issuing a correction. Tank asked whether I would use it before or after we had heard the speech. I said the implementation details were still being negotiated.

Then the game made time part of a puzzle rather than a service for preserving my reputation. Shawna watched how the rules changed between places. Rosa noticed the memories and regrets woven into the journey. Mercedes pointed out that undoing an action and understanding it were not the same thing. I had been hoping they were. We tried a movement again, this time with a different idea. It was satisfying, but it still required the movement. The rewind did not transform my hands into a committee. I recorded that distinction before my enthusiasm could accidentally turn it into a promise.

### The actual attraction

Braid, Anniversary Edition combines platform movement with puzzles built around unusual behavior of time. Its story and images concern memory, desire and regret. The new edition also includes extensive creator commentary, offering another route into the design for someone interested in how a game thinks.

### The catch worth knowing

Rewinding does not remove jumping, hazards or required movement timing. The developer explicitly describes platforming and changing time mechanics. This is a conditional action-puzzle detour, not a standard no-rush fit. Check movement, jump combinations and rewind holds separately; a forgiving recovery system can reduce failure cost without reducing the action itself.

### An opening invitation

Try one introductory movement puzzle and the rewind control. If the physical action is unwelcome, consider the design commentary as an interest to investigate rather than forcing play.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

The minister may revise a mistaken move, but the laws of time decline to erase every embarrassing claim of competence.

Shawna suggested this was not a substitute for examining the lower connection. The lower connection chose that moment to prove her point.

The little living pieces made construction feel less like drawing a line and more like persuading a crowd to hold hands in a dangerous shape. Rosa wanted them to arrive somewhere useful. Mercedes wanted me to stop naming unfinished bridges after myself. I tried a shorter structure. It held a little longer. Another adjustment changed the balance. Soon we were watching cause and effect rather than defending a glorious silhouette. The company in the game appeared to have its own questionable priorities. ### The actual attraction

World of Goo turns drag-and-drop construction into a physics puzzle with a comic corporate world around it. The primary description explicitly says drag and drop. Holding and positioning the pointer may therefore be a central barrier. Physics unfolds while structures are running, and hazards vary by level, so do not assume an unlimited-waiting solution from the word puzzle. Build the smallest useful connection in an early level and watch it behave. Test selecting, dragging and releasing one piece before attempting an elaborate tower.

## A Quiet Board Can Contain a Loud Problem

At the next meeting I displayed two pictures. One was a cheerful creature. The other was an ornate machine surrounded by impressive shadows. I asked the committee which was easier. Shawna asked, 'Easier to move, easier to understand, easier to read, or easier to stop thinking about?' This was an unreasonable number of useful questions for a presentation that had originally contained only two slides.

Tank said the creature could require a difficult sequence of moves. Mercedes said the machine might be lovely to inspect even if she needed a clue. Rosa said neither picture told us whether somebody had to hold a button while doing something else. I looked at my labels, which read FRIENDLY and IMPORTANT. They did not survive the discussion.

We replaced the labels with descriptions of actions. Moving one square. Turning an object. Holding and dragging a piece. Reading a clue. Remembering which room contained a symbol. Waiting for a safe moment to pass a guard. These were not interchangeable demands, and none of them could be inferred reliably from the amount of gold trim on the screen. The book's quieter page design suddenly seemed more useful than my attempted hierarchy of seriousness.

I wanted to preserve one grand distinction: a grown-up evening could include simple play without needing to apologize for it. Mercedes agreed, provided we also acknowledged the opposite. A person who wanted complicated characters should not be handed a coloring toy merely because its controls were small. Taste was part of the decision. We returned both pictures to the table with better descriptions. The creature kept its dignity. The machine lost its automatic promotion. I lost a slide deck, which everyone described as an improvement.

We wrote down what was known from a manual or an exact-edition source and what still needed checking. A source gap stayed a source gap. A scan we could locate but not read did not become a control audit because I looked solemn beside it. I offered to issue a provisional seal. Shawna said the plain sentence about the gap was already doing more useful work than the seal would.

Tank found the red stapler in the cabinet and asked whether I intended to classify it as a historical pointing device. I said its original firmware was still intact. He stapled two loose sheets, demonstrating compatibility with paper. That was the strongest hands-on evidence produced during the ceremony. We left the cabinet open, placed the useful notes beside it, and agreed that an old favorite should earn its place through present enjoyment, with history allowed to make the invitation warmer.

## The Last Compartment

By the end of the department's review, the cabinet contained fewer things than I had intended and more things we could actually find. The gold stamp had a small space of its own. The red stapler sat where a hand could reach it. The binder had become several manageable volumes. Tank opened the door and closed it again without holding the entire structure steady, which I described as a major infrastructure milestone. He described it as a cabinet.

We did not finish every puzzle in order to justify their existence. Some pages made somebody curious immediately. Others revealed a catch that settled the choice just as usefully. A drag could be unwelcome. A dense logic board could be exactly the right invitation. A beautifully told mystery could still include timing that ruled it out for a particular evening. The point of the collection was to make those differences visible before my enthusiasm tried to run over them with a ceremonial trolley.

Mercedes chose a page because she liked the premise, then checked the burden before considering the first action. Rosa set the tea where nobody had to reach across the book. Shawna left a blank line beside an unresolved control question. Tank put the spare fittings in a small bag and wrote CABINET on it. I admired the economy of that label while privately considering whether Cabinet Affairs would sound more established.

There was one compartment left. I proposed reserving it for future expansion. Mercedes suggested leaving it empty until something worth keeping appeared. This felt dangerously unambitious until I noticed how calm the shelf looked without a speculative monument to my plans. An empty place was not a failure to choose. It was room for the next thing that actually appealed.

I put away the final form. Nobody had signed it, and nothing important had gone wrong. The guest could pick a puzzle, return to a story, try a creative toy, or close the book. The cabinet did not award points for finishing its contents. Neither, after a brief and unsuccessful attempt to add a scoring drawer, did I. Tank shut the door. For once the department's most convincing achievement was that everything useful remained easy to reach.


## Volume 5: The Office of People Who Have Something to Tell You

Character stories, dialogue and consequential choices


## The Listening Fee Nobody Agreed To

I opened the Office of People Who Have Something to Tell You with a gold microphone, six upholstered chairs, and a sign that said I UNDERSTAND. The sign cost more than the chairs. That was deliberate. A chair supports one person. My understanding was intended to support an entire market.

Shawna arrived carrying the enormous game binder against her hip. The spine had developed the soft, defeated curve of a man asked to attend another meeting. She set it on the table. One chair rose half an inch. Tank caught the coffee before my administration could baptize its own documents.

“Why is there a microphone?” Shawna asked.

“So nobody feels unheard.”

She touched the switch. My voice came out of a speaker in the bathroom.

“They hear you flushing,” Tank said.

I explained that this was a pilot arrangement. Mercedes explained that every arrangement I did not understand was apparently a pilot, and that I should stop allowing aircraft into the furniture budget. Rosa unplugged the speaker. Instantly the room became capable of conversation.

The proposal was simple. People in these games had jobs, friends, secrets, ambitions, terrible employers, and requests that did not fit into a yes-or-no box. We would listen. We would notice whose company felt interesting. We would find a story worth sitting inside. I had expanded that proposal into a department with a Director of Emotional Weather because a title that good should not have to wait for an actual vacancy.

Rosa read the first sheet. “You have charged a handling fee for feelings.”

“Fictional fee.”

“It has your bank details.”

“Fictional ambition.”

She drew one line through the amount and another through the account number. I admired her commitment to clarity even as she dismantled my retirement. Then she placed a bowl of oranges where the invoice tray had been. Everybody understood the new system immediately. You took an orange. You could talk. You could also remain completely quiet, an option my draft had neglected to monetize.

Mercedes sat in the chair facing the window. “Some of these stories are sad.”

“We have tissues.”

“That isn't a recommendation.”

She was right. A box of tissues did not make every story the right company for every evening. A game about grief might be welcome because it took grief seriously. On another evening the same game might feel like somebody delivering a piano through your chest. The reader got to decide. My stamp, though gold and offensively heavy, could not settle the matter.

Tank opened the binder at a page covered in resource bars. “This one wants me to manage food.”

“Narrative food.”

“I'm already managing real food. Rosa got the last orange.”

Here was the second distinction I had tried to bury beneath stationery: a game could wait for a decision and still make that decision feel pressured. A calendar, a dwindling supply, a political bargain, a friendship with consequences—none required quick fingers to produce a tight stomach. We needed to say what the demands actually were. We also needed to let a perfectly good game remain a perfectly good game that somebody did not want tonight.

Shawna moved my microphone to the end of the table. “And a lot of reading is still a lot of reading.”

I put that on the board. Under it I wrote NO EXAM AFTERWARD. Tank added UNLESS AIDEN HAS LOST HIS KEYS AGAIN. The keys were in my pocket. I made a point of finding them silently.

The reader's chair was not at the end of the table beneath the seal. Rosa put it wherever the reader wanted. Mercedes turned the screen so other people could follow without leaning across whoever held the controls. We agreed that a shared story did not automatically become a multiplayer game. Sometimes one person clicked while the others offered theories. Sometimes everybody shut up so a line could land. Sometimes the best contribution was bringing water and not explaining the joke.

I tried the microphone again. Nothing happened. I checked the cord, the switch, the speaker, and the magnificent little green light. Shawna watched me conduct a full technical inquiry.

“It's unplugged,” she said.

“I know.”

“Then why keep talking into it?”

Because, privately, I had been hoping my position would supply electricity.

We opened the first page without amplification. The tiny red stapler rested beside the oranges. For once it was not holding together a policy. It was keeping the window from knocking shut.

“You're supposed to tell me your problem first,” I said. “Then I prepare the appropriate drink.”

“My problem is a rope in front of the sugar.”

Shawna watched me remove it. On the screen, Coffee Talk offered a counter in an alternate Seattle where fantasy people had recognizable troubles. I immediately proposed separate departments for elves, orcs, and people who ordered something complicated. Rosa replaced all three signs with CUSTOMERS.

I brewed a cup of real coffee and began explaining how its bitterness represented the customer's journey. Mercedes tasted it. “It represents you forgetting the water.” She made another pot while I listened to a fictional conversation that managed, without a velvet rope, to be interesting. The office's first successful intervention was letting somebody else use the kitchen.

## P162 - Coffee Talk Episode 2: Hibiscus & Butterfly

The Second Cup Has Retained Counsel

P162  /  CONDITIONAL  /  pointer input  /  pace: unknown  /  native co-op: unknown

### Edition considered

PC sequel

When the café returned with hibiscus and butterfly pea, I announced a botanical expansion of the listening office. I put a fern behind reception. It had no duties, but its posture suggested excellent discretion.

“That's plastic,” Rosa said.

“Then it won't repeat anything.”

Tank arrived with two cups bearing identical elaborate swirls. I praised his mastery of latte art. He turned one over. It was a saucer. The artistic department had been operating at an angle.

The sequel's customers already had lives before I entered the room. That offended my instinct to introduce everybody, but Mercedes insisted that catching up was a pleasure of returning. Shawna found me preparing an official welcome speech for a fictional barista who had done the job before.

She crossed out the speech and wrote LISTEN FIRST. I drew a heart in the margin. It looked like a dented radish. The plastic fern declined to comment, maintaining the strongest professional record of anybody I had hired that day.

### The actual attraction

## The Committee for Leaving a Sentence Alone

By the middle of the afternoon, I had developed a method for responding to every fictional confession. I leaned forward, lowered my voice, and said, “That must be difficult.” It worked beautifully until Tank told me the printer was out of paper.

“That must be difficult,” I said.

“It must be loaded.”

Mercedes slid a sheet toward me. She had kept a record of my contributions. The same six words appeared seventeen times. At the bottom she had drawn my microphone inside a recycling bin. The drawing was very good. I considered commissioning a portrait before I understood the insult.

“Listening isn't waiting to deploy your sentence,” she said.

I wanted to object on professional grounds. Unfortunately the profession was one I had invented that morning, and its entire training consisted of buying the sign. Shawna took the page and folded it into a small stand for the screen. My empty reassurance was finally providing practical support.

We had reached the games where choices mattered in different ways. Some changed a line. Some spent a resource. Some opened or closed a route. Some simply let you express how a character regarded a thing that would happen anyway. None of these automatically made the others dishonest. The pleasure could be in steering events, in inhabiting a voice, or in noticing a detail that another person missed.

I proposed a universal significance scale. Rosa asked whether I could accurately scale the amount of tea left in my cup. I lifted it to demonstrate. It was empty. She refilled it without comment, which was both kind and a devastating audit.

Tank had found a conversation he wanted to finish. The rest of us were halfway through a discussion of what he should say. He raised one hand.

“Can I hear what they say first?”

We could. We did. The room became quieter than my expensive acoustic panels had ever managed to make it. A fictional person finished a thought. Tank chose a response none of us had suggested. It belonged to his reading of the scene, and nobody needed to win an argument about it.

That gave Shawna the policy she had wanted all day: suggestions by invitation. A companion could read aloud if asked. A companion could keep a note if wanted. A companion could solve a menu problem without taking over the character's life. If a difficult choice was part of the fun, we did not steal it in the name of being helpful.

I approved the policy with the gold stamp. The ink pad was dry.

“You can just say yes,” Rosa said.

I said yes. It left no embossed border, no seal, no glorious mark that future historians could place beneath glass. Everybody understood me anyway.

Outside the window, the wind changed. The red stapler held. My Director of Emotional Weather had produced less useful weather management than a stationery item with one visible screw.

P191  /  CONDITIONAL  /  pointer input  /  pace: unknown  /  native co-op: unknown

### Edition considered

PC base game; Kingdom of Rizia expansion separate

I assumed the presidency of the office with a mandate to improve everything, especially the view of my portrait from the doorway. My first cabinet meeting concerned whether the snack budget should fund snacks or a photograph of me funding snacks.

Rosa voted for food. Tank voted for a chair that stopped making the sound of a submarine dying. Shawna placed three mutually incompatible promises in front of me and asked which one I intended to break. I requested a fourth promise, preferably one about listening.

Suzerain arrived with an entire country attached to the question. My campaign smile suddenly looked like something a printer had produced on its economy setting. People had interests, memories, livelihoods. They were not blank spaces waiting for my inspirational handwriting.

Mercedes removed the portrait. This improved the view considerably. I announced a historic transparency initiative and spent the remainder of the meeting admitting that I had not read the second page.

### The actual attraction

As President Anton Rayne, you navigate Sordland's political institutions, economy, international relationships and family life. The attraction is sustained argument: people want incompatible things, and a policy decision can become a personal problem later.

### The catch worth knowing

This is a dense reading and decision game with a codex, reports and many relationships to remember. Political violence and harsh outcomes add pressure. The inspected description does not establish every input's timing or hold requirements. This card covers the base game, excluding the Rizia expansion.

### An opening invitation

Read the opening political setup and one conversation. Judge whether identifying factions feels intriguing or like an unwanted meeting. A companion can discuss priorities without supplying the supposedly correct ideology.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

Approved for the evening you want to interrogate a minister; my portrait has already exercised its right to remain silent.

Tank needed a working kettle. Rosa needed the snack cupboard replenished. Mercedes requested that I stop calling the hallway my northern province. I granted all three requests verbally and then discovered that verbal grants do not heat water.

In Yes, Your Grace, a king must hear requests while his resources remain inconveniently finite. I regarded this as a terrible design flaw in monarchy. Shawna regarded it as the beginning of accounting.

She placed four coins beside six requests and watched me arrange them into a more optimistic shape. The coins remained four. Eventually I climbed down from the throne to help with the kettle. Both chairs immediately became more useful, which Tank said was my strongest policy result.

I demonstrated my new surveillance department by projecting my own browser history onto the office wall. It included searches for ceremonial cape storage, affordable marble that looks expensive, and whether a person can trademark the concept of arriving.

'Strong case,' Tank said.

I explained that these fragments had been cruelly separated from context. Shawna asked what context could possibly improve the marble query. I said architectural ambition. Mercedes said plastic.

Orwell made the exercise less comfortable. A digital trace can be true and still create the wrong picture when somebody chooses which piece to pass along. My department's excellent equipment consisted of a mouse, a projector and an alarming amount of confidence in itself.

Rosa switched off the projector before the cape measurements appeared. I thanked her for respecting due process. She said she was respecting everyone else's lunch. We agreed that access to a detail does not automatically make its use wise.

You investigate digital records for a state surveillance program and decide what information to submit. The pleasure lies in connecting messages, profiles and apparent contradictions, then noticing how your selection shapes somebody else's fate.

Orwell: Ignorance is Strength understands that territory: information gains power through its placement, selection and use. I tried writing a correction that celebrated my commitment to corrections. Mercedes crossed out everything except RETURNED A CHAIR.

Tank wanted to know why the public needed a statement about a chair at all. This was a devastating question for an institution with an entire stationery drawer devoted to statements.

Rosa moved the drawer out of the kitchen and asked us to set the table. For once, a useful event occurred without a headline. I privately described it as a successful pilot of our low-visibility domestic infrastructure initiative, but I had the decency to keep that inside my head.

## The Office Closes When the Guest Leaves

I had reserved the final hour for a grand address on the transformative power of narrative. The heading occupied half a page. Beneath it I had written REMEMBER TO BE HUMBLE in letters large enough to be read from the corridor.

Mercedes found it while looking for a clean sheet.

“You scheduled humility?”

“Otherwise it gets crowded out.”

“By what?”

I glanced at the microphone. She nodded as if that completed the paperwork.

Instead of the address, Rosa asked everybody what they actually wanted to return to. The answers were not a ranking. One person wanted another conversation at a counter. Another wanted a difficult political decision precisely because it was difficult. Somebody wanted a strange town. Somebody else had had enough reading and wanted dinner. Nobody had failed the afternoon by choosing dinner.

Tank named one character he liked and one menu he disliked. That was more useful than my twelve-column experience matrix. Shawna wrote the two observations on a small card. We could act on them. We could try a different game, make a short next-step note, or return another day. We did not need to invent a permanent identity for a person from one evening's taste.

I looked at the binder. Forty stories sat in this part of it, and each contained more lives, choices, and complications than any summary could comfortably hold. The book could point toward a door. It could tell you whether there appeared to be stairs behind it. It could admit when we had not inspected the stairs. It could not make you enjoy the room.

That was a smaller job than the one I had advertised. It was also an honest one.

Shawna began stacking the chairs. I stopped her after the second.

“Leave one.”

She waited.

“If somebody wants to keep going.”

We left the screen where it was, the water beside it, and the route note within reach. There was no ceremony of completion. A conversation could remain unfinished without becoming a debt. A game could be abandoned without insulting its creator. The office could still contain an open book after the person who opened it had decided to do something else.

Rosa handed me the sign. I UNDERSTAND still glittered across it in deeply unnecessary gold.

“What do you want to do with this?”

I turned it over. The back was plain white. With a marker, I wrote TELL ME MORE.

Mercedes studied it. “Better.”

Tank added OR DON'T beneath it.

That was the part that made it work.

We carried the oranges into the kitchen. I took the gold microphone with us because it turned out to be an excellent weight for holding down the corner of a curling recipe. My understanding finally had a practical assignment. It did not speak once.

The red stapler stayed at the window until the evening cooled. Then Shawna closed the window, picked it up, and put it beside the binder. Nothing was permanently fixed. Everything necessary remained within reach.


## Volume 6: The Joint Commission for Bringing a Friend

Native cooperative games, shared puzzles and stories for an optional companion


## Two Controllers, One Astonishingly Bad Contract

I bought a second controller and announced that loneliness had been defeated. Tank asked where the batteries were. I told him he was focusing on implementation instead of vision. He put the controller down. My victory lasted eleven seconds.

The Joint Commission for Bringing a Friend met at the same table that had survived the other five volumes. Survived was the right word. Its legs had begun making small noises of legal significance. The game binder lay across the middle like a bridge that had been designed by a man who hated rivers. Beside it sat the gold stamp, the tiny red stapler, and a plate of sandwiches Rosa had expressly excluded from all jurisdiction.

I had prepared seats for Player One and Player Two. Player One's seat reclined. Player Two's was a folding chair with a sticker that said AUXILIARY.

Shawna peeled off the sticker.

“Everybody gets a comfortable chair.”

“The design expresses roles.”

“So does a throne. We aren't putting one in the living room.”

Tank looked toward the hallway. I looked somewhere else. The delivery men would be informed.

The reader had asked, at various points, whether a game could be enjoyed together. I had heard an opportunity to establish a commission. Those were not the same request. Mercedes made me write the ordinary question on the first page: what would each person actually do?

That exposed a surprising number of differences. In some games, two people each controlled a character. In some, one character had more abilities than the other. In others, players needed separate devices and had to describe information neither could see in full. Some games offered distinct cooperative levels rather than a shared version of the main story. And some had no native cooperative mode at all; two people could still enjoy discussing a puzzle while one operated the controls.

I had labelled all of these TWO FRIENDS, EXCELLENT. Shawna returned the page with the kind of silence that means a document has disappointed a woman personally.

“We need the distinction,” she said.

“We have a distinction. Excellent is in italics.”

Rosa took the marker from me.

We made three plain signs. Two people controlling the game. Two people sharing ideas around one player's game. A game for somebody who specifically wanted its action demands. The last sign was necessary because friendly art could conceal a lot of running, jumping, aiming, holding, and falling off things. A knitted character could demand excellent timing while looking as if it had personally forgiven you.

Tank recovered the batteries from the drawer marked HISTORICAL RECORDS. The records were mostly spare cables and a receipt for the sign that said I UNDERSTAND. He installed them in the second controller. I watched him perform the practical miracle my announcement had skipped.

“Now,” he said, “who's doing what?”

That became our opening question for every shared game. A helpful friend could handle a menu or read a clue, if invited. A helpful friend could also become a second weather system, shouting instructions while pushing the pace. We agreed to describe before commanding, ask before taking over, and leave space for the person holding the controls to make the interesting decision.

Mercedes tested the arrangement by handing me a sandwich.

“Eat this cooperatively.”

I held one side. Tank held the other. We looked at each other for a while.

“This is stupid,” he said.

“Some activities have a natural lead.”

Rosa cut the sandwich in half. It was the first redesign of the day to improve everybody's experience without a presentation.

The native-co-op label would describe a feature, not a social obligation. Nobody owed us a second player. Nobody needed permission to enjoy a game alone. Where cooperative support depended on an edition, a platform, an update, or a particular mode, we would say so. The original Secret of Mana and its later remake were not one interchangeable object merely because their names matched. My filing cabinet had tried that trick with three different insurance policies. It had not survived contact with Shawna.

We also needed to distinguish a first trial from a promise about the whole game. A pleasant opening could introduce later timers, fights, menus, or demanding movement. Our invitation was to test one useful thing: a conversation between players, a room, a small build, a puzzle, a handoff. We were not scheduling anybody's endurance.

I stamped the revised charter. This time the ink worked. Unfortunately I had placed the charter on the sandwich plate.

“You certified the bread,” Tank said.

I turned the slice over. JOINT COMMISSION shone blue across the crust.

Mercedes leaned back in her comfortable chair. “At least that one actually supports two people.”

We ate the unstamped halves and opened the binder. The second controller remained available. It did not acquire authority over anybody by being present.


## P201 - Secret of Mana

The Sword Requires Fewer Speeches and More Attention

P201  /  CONDITIONAL  /  directional input  /  pace: real time  /  native co-op: yes

### Edition considered

1993 SNES original / Collection of Mana on Switch; not 2018 remake

I lifted a cardboard sword and announced that the three of us would restore balance to the world. Tank asked whether balance included moving the binder away from the edge of the table. I said the quest had many layers.

Secret of Mana gave us forests, companions and monsters that did not wait for my speeches. I attempted to negotiate with the first creature by explaining our cooperative values. It appeared unmoved by the committee's mission statement.

Shawna reached for the ring menu. For a moment, the action stopped and we could think. I immediately proposed a subcommittee on swords. She closed the menu before I found a chairperson.

Rosa clarified that playing together meant each person actually wanted to fight, explore and manage the party. It did not mean Tank performed every difficult action while I called it leadership. We set down the cardboard sword and agreed to try one familiar area, with both voices carrying more weight than my heroic introduction.

### The actual attraction

The original 1993 adventure offers a colourful party journey with native cooperative fighting once companions join. Its overhead view removes free-camera steering, while towns, equipment, magic and connected areas give the journey continuity.

### The catch worth knowing

Combat runs in real time. Original manual instructions include held dashing and charged attacks, plus Y opening the paused ring menu. Original SNES two-player play needs two controllers; a third needs a multiplayer adapter. Collection of Mana supports up to three. The 2018 remake is excluded.

### An opening invitation

Enjoy the solo opening together or use an existing shared save after an ally joins. Try moving, opening the ring and one familiar encounter. The second player should choose their own role rather than automatically receiving every difficult job.

### Aiden’s entirely unnecessary ruling

A conditional retro expedition for two willing adventurers; my cardboard sword qualifies only as evidence that packaging can develop delusions.

### Evidence desk

Nintendo Collection of Mana page confirms original Secret of Mana supports 1–3 players. Original manual text inspected through archival transcription establishes ring pause, held actions and SNES controller/adapter rules. Ally-gated joining follows party availability. No 2018 PC remake metadata is used.

The Ferry's Emotional Baggage Exceeds My Luggage Allowance

P202  /  CONDITIONAL  /  directional input  /  pace: mixed  /  native co-op: yes

### Edition considered

PC Farewell Edition

I designed a passenger ferry with a ballroom, a portrait gallery and an extremely small place for passengers. Rosa asked where anyone would sleep. I pointed to a future expansion marked COMFORT, PENDING PRESTIGE.

Spiritfarer had a better order of priorities. People arrived carrying needs, histories and the knowledge that a journey could end. My proposed Captain's Welcome was seven pages long. Tank used page four to level the model boat.

Daffodil the cat had my immediate respect: excellent presence, no committee membership required. I announced that I would take the cat role and supervise from an attractive ledge. Shawna suggested actually testing what the two roles could do before allocating our entire friendship.

We cooked one imaginary meal and removed the ballroom from the plan. Mercedes kept the window seat. The boat was smaller, more useful and considerably less impressed with me. I found that I could bear this, particularly when somebody passed me the bread.

Build and travel on a ferry, care for spirits, gather materials and eventually say goodbye. The Farewell Edition includes the expanded character stories. Native local cooperation pairs Stella with Daffodil, giving two people a shared place to inhabit.

Movement includes jumping and gliding; chores, recipes and resources accumulate. The cat's role differs from Stella's. Meet a passenger, walk around the boat and try one chore in each role. Check whether both people enjoy what their character can do. Leave the larger building plan alone until the small shared activity feels worthwhile.

For company that wants to care for a floating household; the committee has denied my application to become Admiral of the Pantry.

## The Friend Who Became a Tutorial

I discovered an extraordinary gift for cooperative instruction. Whatever Tank was doing, I could describe how I would have done it three seconds earlier. This service required no controller and, apparently, no correct information.

“Go left,” I said.

He went left.

“Your other left.”

Tank paused the discussion by looking at me. It was not a pause button; it was a face. The distinction mattered because I had already become confident enough to promise features I had not checked.

Shawna put a small card beside the screen. It read ASK.

“Ask what?”

“Whether he wants help.”

“He clearly needs—”

She tapped the card.

I asked. Tank said he wanted to try the thing. So I let him try the thing. A door opened. I immediately felt the urge to explain that his method had been broadly consistent with my strategy. Mercedes placed a sandwich directly in my hand. It was a merciful intervention.

Later, in a different puzzle, Tank asked what I could see. I described a shape, its position, and the marks beside it. He compared those with his own information. For once my mouth was connected to an actual function. We made progress by building a shared description, not by making one person into the other's remote-control accessory.

Rosa noticed the difference first. “You two sound like you're enjoying it.”

I wanted to issue a certificate. Shawna pointed at the ASK card. I did not ask whether they wanted a certificate because some impulses deserve to be carried privately until they pass.

We adjusted the room instead. The screen moved so both people could see. A lamp stopped reflecting across a clue. The water came closer. None of that changed the software, and none magically erased a game's real demands. It simply removed three problems I had accidentally supplied for free.

The binder contained some excellent candidates for shared thinking that were still single-player games. We kept that plain. Taking turns at a puzzle could be enjoyable, but it was not the same as two simultaneous characters. Watching a friend play could be a pleasure, but it was not automatically participation. A guest could prefer to watch, offer a theory, take a turn, or go make tea. The room could accommodate all four without assigning ranks.

The red stapler disappeared during this discussion. I suspected theft. Rosa found it beneath my elbow.

“You are the obstacle in more systems than you realize,” she said.

I returned it to its tray. Then I moved my elbow, my advice, and my magnificent self far enough back that the next person could reach the controls.
